Sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes system on the Heisenberg group: Weak solutions, well-posedness and smoothing effects
Adrien TENDANI-SOLER

Abstract
This article is devoted to the derivation of the incompressible sub-Riemannian Euler and the sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes systems, and the analysis of the last one in the case of the Heisenberg group. In contrast to the classical Navier-Stokes system in the Euclidean setting, the diffusion is not elliptic but only hypoelliptic, and the commutator of the Leray projector and the hypoelliptic Laplacian is of order two. Yet, we study the existence of solutions in two different settings: within the L 2 L^{2} setting which provides global existence of weak solutions; within a critical scale-invariant Sobolev-type space, associated with the regularity of the generators of the first stratum of the Lie algebra of right-invariant vector fields. In this latter class, we establish global existence of solutions for small data and a stability estimate in the energy spaces which ensures the uniqueness of the solutions in this class. Furthermore, we show in this setting that these solutions instantly become analytic in the vertical direction. Surprisingly, we obtain a larger lower bound of the radius of analyticity in the vertical direction for large times than for the usual incompressible Navier-Stokes system in the Euclidean setting. Finally, using the structure of the system, we recover the 𝒞 ∞ \mathcal{C}^{\infty} smoothness in the other directions by using the analyticity in the vertical variable.



The author is partially supported by the Project TRECOS ANR-20-CE40-0009 funded by the ANR. This work benefited from the financial support of the University of Bordeaux within an initiative of excellence, under the "France 2030" plan.

1
Introduction

In this article, we derived a new class of anisotropic models for incompressible fluids, called the sub-Riemannian Euler and Navier-Stokes system, and we investigate the Cauchy theory of the latter one in the case of the Heisenberg group. We study three problems in the case of the Heisenberg group: the global existence of finite energy (weak) solutions, the global well-posedness in a Sobolev-type critical framework and the (analytic and
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
) regularity of the solutions. Before stating our results and methods, let us introduce the models.

1.1
Presentation of the model

Many PDEs from fluid mechanics are anisotropic as, for instance, Prandtl system, Ekman layer for rotating fluids, or models of the wind-driven oceanic circulation and liquid-crystal models (see for instance
[
35
]
Chapters 4 and 5 and
[
38
]
Chapter 4 for more examples). The mathematical analysis of these equations presents several challenging questions about the properties of their solutions. On the other hand, investigating classical PDEs (such as the Laplace equation, heat equation, wave equation and Schrödinger equation) within the context of the sub-Riemannian geometry, by considering their subelliptic counterpart, has recently attracted considerable attention (see for instance
[
9
]
,
[
22
]
and
[
32
]
). This has led to a reappraisal of the usual properties of their solutions and the development of new approaches. This work is at the intersection of these two topics: we consider anisotropic models, describing incompressible fluids on the whole space, whose anisotropy is naturally encoded by a sub-Riemannian structure.

Motivations.

We aim to write a class of equations which describes the motion of a fluid when the velocity field is anisotropic and, more precisely, cannot take all possible directions. Keeping in mind that geometrically, a vector field is a section of the tangent bundle, we are naturally led to investigate the situation in which the anisotropy comes from the velocity field being a section (called horizontal vector fields) of a subbundle (called horizontal bundle) of the tangent bundle. The sub-Riemannian geometry provides a natural context to describe such situations. In this geometric framework, the choice of a horizontal velocity field translates into the fact that particles of fluid cannot move in all directions according to predefined restrictions. In this work, the choice of the Heisenberg group is led by the fact that this is the simplest non-trivial example of a sub-Riemannian structure on the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Formal derivation of the sub-Riemannian Euler and the sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes systems on the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
.

In this paragraph, we present the model and its derivation, and the underlying group structure will be given in detail in Section
2
.
We begin by defining what we will call a horizontal vector field on the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
.
Let us consider a vector field
v:=\!^{t}(v_{1},\dots,v_{2d+1})
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
, where for any
i\in[\![1,2d+1]\!]
,
v_{i}:(Y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto v_{i}(Y,s)\in\mathbb{R}
. We say that
v
is a
horizontal vector field
if the following relation holds

v=\mathcal{R}\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{2d}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{2d},\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
is a map given, for any
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, by

\mathcal{R}_{Y}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}I_{2d}\\
\hline\cr{}^{t}(\mathfrak{S}Y)\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\text{ with }\mathfrak{S}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&2I_{d}\\
\hline\cr-2I_{d}&0\\
\end{array}\right).

(1.1)

(Here,
I_{p}
denotes the identity matrix of size
p
.)
In other words,
v:\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
is a horizontal vector field if

\forall(y,\eta,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R},\quad v_{2d+1}(y,\eta,s)=2\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(\eta_{j}v_{j}(y,\eta,s)-y_{j}v_{j+d}(y,\eta,s)\right).

Because a horizontal vector field is determined by its
2d
-first coordinates, we slightly abuse the notation by calling maps
v:\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2d}
horizontal vector fields in the following, meaning that the component of
\mathcal{R}v
is defined as above.

Using
\mathcal{R}
, we can define the following (left-invariant, see Section
2
) differential operators

\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}:=\!^{t}\mathcal{R}\nabla,\ \ \divergence_{\mathbb{H}}:=\divergence(\mathcal{R}\cdot)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}:=\divergence(\mathcal{R}\,\!^{t}\mathcal{R}\nabla\cdot).

(1.2)

See also (
2.5
) for another definition of those operators in terms of generators of the Lie algebra of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
.

The leading idea of our derivation of the sub-Riemannian Euler or Navier-Stokes system is to
describe the dynamic of a fluid in the Euclidean space for which the velocity field is horizontal
. To derive the Euler system, we follow the usual derivation of the Euler system on
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
, but we further impose that the trajectories of the fluids particles follow a horizontal velocity field. In the following, the velocity field of the particles will be considered to be a smooth function
u:(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}\mapsto u(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

Incompressibility.
We assume that the horizontal vector field
\mathcal{R}u
is incompressible, that is, with the operator introduced in (
1.2
)

\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0.

Lagrangian description.
The motion of a particle of fluid with horizontal velocity field
u
is described by the flow map
\Psi
defined as follows

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\Psi(t,\cdot)=\mathcal{R}u(t,\Psi(t,\cdot)),\\
\Psi(0,\cdot)=I_{{2d+1}}.\end{cases}

The variation of the velocity of a particle of the fluid which moves on
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
is then given by

\frac{d}{dt}u_{\Psi}(t,x)=\left(\partial_{t}u+\mathcal{R}u\cdot\nabla u\right)(t,\Psi(t,x)),

where
u_{\Psi}(t,x):=u(t,\Psi(t,x))
with
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
. By using (
1.2
), we deduce that

\frac{d}{dt}u_{\Psi}(t,x)=\left(\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\right)(t,\Psi(t,x)).

According to the Newton laws, the term
\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
is the sum of the forces acting on the fluid.

Pressure.
We follow the physical construction of the pressure term in the Euler and Navier-Stokes system on the Euclidean setting by keeping in mind that the flow is horizontal, see for instance
[
29
]
. Given a volume of fluid
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
, we assume that the pressure acts on
\partial\Omega
with respect to the horizontal normal
\overrightarrow{n}_{\mathbb{H}}:=\!^{t}\mathcal{R}\overrightarrow{n}
, where
\overrightarrow{n}
is the outward-pointing unit normal vector field on
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
, since the fluid moves through the boundary
\partial\Omega
following horizontal velocity fields. Thus the action of the pressure on the fluid domain
\Omega
is given by the term
-\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}p
.

At this step we obtain the
sub-Riemannian Euler equations on the Heisenberg group

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u=-\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}p,\\
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0.\end{cases}

This system formally satisfies the conservation of the kinetic energy: for any sufficiently smooth solutions
(u,p)
of the sub-Riemannian Euler system, we have

\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=0.

The
sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes equations on the Heisenberg group
are the following viscous perturbation of the preceding system, perturbed by the Heisenberg sub-Laplacian:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u=-\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}p,\\
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0,\end{cases}

(1.3)

so that for any sufficiently smooth solutions
(u,p)
of (
1.3
), we have the dissipation of the kinetic energy according to the law

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=0.

(1.4)

The choice of the operators
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
is driven by the fact that this operator is the simplest natural diffusion in the geometric setting of the Heisenberg group (see
[
11
]
Chapter 2, Section 5.4 or Subsection
2.1
in this article), and it also guarantees the above dissipation of the energy.

Note that there are other models of incompressible Navier-Stokes systems on the Heisenberg group, in particular in
[
33
]
, but we think that our model is more physically justified than the one in
[
33
]
, for which there is no clear dissipation law such as (
1.4
) - in fact, the corresponding Euler equation in
[
33
]
does not preserve the kinetic energy of the fluid.

Comment on the geometric aspects.

We now describe the geometric framework underlying the above derivation of the sub-Riemannian Euler and Navier-Stokes systems.
The notion of horizontal vector fields we use is associated with the so-called left-invariant sub-Riemannian structure of the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
identified to
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
with a suitable group law. Note that our derivation of the Sub-Riemannian system on the Heisenberg group can be extended to more general sub-Riemannian manifolds, for instance stratified Lie groups (see Section
9
). The importance of the case of stratified Lie groups comes from the fact that they are model spaces in sub-Riemannian geometry (as the Euclidean spaces are model spaces in
Riemannian geometry).

From the point of view of the mathematical analysis, the importance of stratified Lie groups in sub-Riemannian geometry is illustrated by the results of
[
36
]
. In
[
36
]
, the authors obtained sharp regularity results for the sum of squares of Hörmander vector fields by using analysis on nilpotent Lie groups. A crucial step of their proof is the
construction of new vector fields (on a larger manifold), which lift
the original vector fields and can be locally approximated by left-invariant vector fields on a stratified group.

Also note that homogeneous Lie groups (which include stratified Lie groups) provide a natural setting to generalize many tools from Euclidean harmonic analysis and in particular to define a suitable Fourier transform on (non-compact) locally compact Lie groups (see
[
19
]
and Section
2.2
).

Regarding the Heisenberg sub-Laplacian.

The diffusion induced by the hypoelliptic operator
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
is anisotropic: the diffusion is generated by only
2d
independent vector fields, while
\mathbb{H}^{d}
is of dimension
2d+1
(this implies in particular that
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
is not elliptic). The influence of this viscous perturbation in the sub-Riemannian Euler system on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, through smoothing effect on the solutions of the sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes system on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, will be studied in detail in this article. In particular, we will point out that, in the sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes system on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, the energy is also dissipated in the vertical direction, although it does not appear explicitly in the dissipation law (
1.4
). We will underline the importance of this phenomenon on the regularity of the solutions of (
1.3
).

1.2
Main results: Existence of weak solutions, well-posedness and smoothing effects

Notation 1.2.1
.

For
T>0
,
p\in[1,+\infty)\cup\{\infty\}
and
E
a Banach space, we set

L^{p}_{T}(E):=L^{p}((0,T);E),\ \ L^{p}(E):=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}_{+};E)\ \ \text{and}\ \ L^{p}_{loc}(E):=L^{p}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};E).

In the same spirit, if
f
belongs to
L^{p}_{T}(E)
, respectively to
L^{p}(E)
, we set

\|f\|_{L^{p}_{T}(E)}:=\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|f(t)\|_{E}^{p}dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\ \ \text{respectively}\ \ \|f\|_{L^{p}(E)}:=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|f(t)\|_{E}^{p}dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

We also denote by
\mathcal{C}_{w}([0,T);E)
, respectively
\mathcal{C}_{w}(\mathbb{R}_{+},E)
, the space of measurable functions from
[0,T)
to
E
, respectively from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
E
, which are continuous for the weak topology on
E
. When
F
is a functional space, we use the same notations for
F
and
F^{2d}
.

In this article, we consider the following Cauchy problem:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u=-\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}p\ &\text{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\\
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0\ &\text{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\end{cases}

(1.5)

with the initial condition

u_{|_{t=0}}=u_{0}\ \ \ \text{in}\ \ \mathbb{H}^{d},

(1.6)

where
u_{0}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})^{2d}
and satisfies
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0
. In this article, we investigate two frameworks for the Cauchy problem (
1.5
)-(
1.6
).

Weak solutions in
L^{2}
.

For the incompressible Navier-Stokes system in the Euclidean setting, the question of the existence of the global weak solutions in the energy space, the so called Leray solution, was solved in
[
31
]
. Still, their uniqueness is nowadays largely open despite some recent progress on this question (see
[
15
,
3
]
).

In the case of the sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes, the structure of the system is adapted to obtain the existence of Leray-type solution for (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) with initial data in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Let us first introduce the notion of weak solutions for (
1.5
)-(
1.6
).

Definition 1.2.2

(Definition of the weak solutions)
.

Let us define the space

\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}:=\left\{\varphi\in\mathcal{D}([0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})^{2d}~\middle|~\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi)=0\right\}.

Let
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
be a horizontal vector field satisfying
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0
. We say that a horizontal vector field
u
belonging to
L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
is a
global weak solution
of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) if

1.

(Integrability conditions)
u
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{w}([0,+\infty);L^{2})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{2})
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{2})
,

2.

(Initial condition)
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}u(t)=u_{0}
for the weak topology of
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
,

3.

(Momentum equation) for any
t^{\prime}\leqslant t
in
[0,+\infty)
and for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}_{\sigma}
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}u(t)\cdot\varphi(t)dx-\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}\left(u\cdot\partial_{t}\varphi+u\cdot\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\varphi+(u\otimes u)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\varphi\right)dxd\tau=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}u(t^{\prime})\cdot\varphi(t^{\prime})dx,

4.

(Continuity equation) For all
t>0
, we have
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u(t))=0
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})^{2d}
.

Let us remark that any weak solution has finite energy, since it belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
at any times. Our first main result is the following one, whose proof is given in Section
5
.

Theorem 1.2.3

(Existence of weak solutions)
.

Let
u_{0}
be a horizontal vector field belonging to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and satisfying
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0
. Then there exists a global weak solution
u
of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
), satisfying the following energy estimate

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}(L^{2})}^{2}\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

We extend this result to the sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes system on general stratified Lie group in
9
. The key step of the proof of Theorem
1.2.3
consists of defining a suitable approximate problem.
This approximate problem follows a suitably modified version of the Friedrichs method and involves the Fourier transform on the Heisenberg group. The analysis of the approximate problem reveals technical and conceptual difficulties due to the intrinsic structure of the system (pressure, nonlinear terms and divergence free condition), see Section
3
, and the non-ellipticity of
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
, see Section
4
. As we will see next, this approximation is also used later to establish the existence and the regularity of the solutions of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) in the critical framework presented afterwards.

Well-posedness in the critical framework
\tilde{H}^{d}
.

An important notion to select functional spaces for studying Cauchy problems of PDE is the so-called scaling invariance. It turns out that the Sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes system (
1.5
) on the Heisenberg group also presents a scaling invariance, which is strongly related to the geometric structure of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
(see Section
2
).

Scaling invariance.
The Sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes system (
1.5
) has the following scaling invariance property:
u
satisfies (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) with initial data
u_{0}
if and only if for all
\mu>0
, the horizontal vector field
u_{\mu}
given for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
(Y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{R}
by

u_{\mu}(t,Y,s):=\mu u(\mu^{2}t,\mu Y,\mu^{2}s),

(1.7)

satisfies (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) with data
\mu u_{0}(\mu\cdot,\mu^{2}\cdot)
.

We are thus looking for a Banach space of initial data whose norm is invariant by this scaling transformation. Such space is called a critical space for (
1.5
).

Let us define the space

\tilde{H}^{d}:=\left\{f\in L^{2d+2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})~\middle|~(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\right\},

where the operator
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
is called the right-invariant sub-Laplacian on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
. This operator will be defined in (
2.7
) and its fractional powers will be defined via the functional calculus of
-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
in Section
2.3
. The space
\tilde{H}^{d}
is a critical space for (
1.5
) (see (
2.24
)).
Our second main result is the following Theorem on the Cauchy problem of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) in
\tilde{H}^{d}
:

Theorem 1.2.4

(Global well-posedness in
\tilde{H}^{d}
)
.

Let
d\geqslant 1
be an integer.

1.

(Existence and uniqueness) For any small enough horizontal vector field
u_{0}
in
\tilde{H}^{d}
satisfying
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0
,
there exists a unique solution
u
of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) which satisfies
u\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d})
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d})
.

2.

(Stability estimate) There exists a positive constant
C
such that for any
T>0
and for any solutions
u
and
v
of (
1.5
) such that
u
and
v
belong to
\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,T];\tilde{H}^{d})
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v
belong to
L^{2}([0,T];\tilde{H}^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\|u-v\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}(

\displaystyle u-v)\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|u(0)-v(0)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\exp{\left(C\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+C\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}\right)}.

Before going further, let us point out that the notion of solutions in Theorem
1.2.4
does not refer to the concept of weak solutions introduced in Definition
1.2.2
, since the solution
u
constructed in Theorem
1.2.4
, Item 1 does not a priori belong to the energy space
L^{\infty}(L^{2})
. Therefore, the solutions constructed in Theorem
1.2.4
are to be understood as follows:
u
satisfies
u\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d})
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d})
,
u(0)=u_{0}
in
\tilde{H}^{d}
, and
u
satisfies (
1.5
) in the sense of distributions (i.e. Definition
1.2.2
, Items 3 and 4).

Theorem
1.2.4
will be proved in Section
6
in two steps. We first show Theorem
6.1.1
, which states the existence of global solutions with small initial data in
\tilde{H}^{d}
. Next, we establish Theorem
6.2.1
, which provides the stability estimate in Item 2. Of course, this stability estimate ensures the uniqueness of the solutions of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) claimed in Theorem
1.2.4
, Item 1.

Let us make some remarks about the regularity of the solutions provided by Theorem
1.2.4
. First, the main advantage of the operator
-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
and its powers is that it commutes with the operators
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}
,
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
, see Section
2
and Section
3
. Second, the diffusion in (
1.5
) corresponds to the left-invariant sub-Laplacian
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
, and is thus distinct from the information that we propagate, that is the
\tilde{H}^{d}
regularity, which corresponds to the right-invariant sub-Laplacian
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
, see Remark
2.4.4
. Accordingly, to obtain strong solutions of (
1.5
), we need to get more regularity on the solutions constructed in Theorem
1.2.4
.

Smoothing effects in the critical framework
\tilde{H}^{d}
.

The problem of the regularity for the solution of the Navier-Stokes system in the Euclidean sitting was mainly investigated since the pioneer work
[
31
]
. The question of the regularity was treated firstly for its link with the uniqueness of the Leray solutions and the analytic smoothing effect was studied later (see
[
20
,
24
,
4
,
16
,
26
,
17
]
). The relevant measure of the analytic smoothing is the notion of the radius of analyticity. In particular the radius of analyticity for the solution of the Navier-Stokes system was studied for its link with the turbulence theory (see for instance
[
25
]
). More applications of the estimate of the radius of analyticity in space for solutions of the Navier-Stokes equation appear in other contexts, such as numerical analysis
[
18
]
, temporal decay rates of Sobolev norms
[
34
]
and geometric regularity criteria for the Navier–Stokes equations
[
23
]
.
In the case of the incompressible Navier-Stokes system in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the best estimate in large times on the radius of analyticity
\rad(u(t)):=\sup\left\{R\geqslant 0~\middle|~e^{R(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup})^{1/2}}u(t)\in\dot{H}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\right\}
of the solutions
u
of the incompressible Navier-Stokes system in the critical framework
\dot{H}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
(see
[
26
]
) is, to the best of our knowledge,

\liminf_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{\rad(u(t))}{\sqrt{t\ln(t)}}>0.

(1.8)

In the following, we investigate whether an analytic smoothing effect occurs for (
1.5
). Before stating our results, let us first introduce the notion of the radius of analyticity that we use in this article.

Radius of analyticity in
\tilde{H}^{d}
in the vertical direction.
For any horizontal vector field
f
that belongs to
\tilde{H}^{d}
, we define the radius of analyticity of
f
with respect to the variable
s
, denoted by
\rad_{s}(f)
, by setting

\rad_{s}(f):=\sup\left\{R\geqslant 0~\middle|~e^{R|D_{s}|}f\in\tilde{H}^{d}\right\}.

(1.9)

We show in Theorem
A.0.1
, that, similarly to the case of homogeneous Sobolev spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see
[
26
]
), if
\rad_{s}(f)\geqslant R>0
then
f=g+h
where
g
belongs to
\tilde{H}^{d}
is an entire function with respect to the variable
s
and
h
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, and can be extended to a holomorphic function with respect to the variable
s
on the strip
\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}~\middle|~|\Im(z)|<R\right\}
.

We obtain the following regularity result:

Theorem 1.2.5

(Regularity of the solution in
\tilde{H}^{d}
)
.

Let
\sigma\in(0,4d)
. Then, for any small enough initial data
u_{0}\in\tilde{H}^{d}
, the associated solution
u
of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) given by Theorem
1.2.4
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
and for any
t>0
and
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
, we have

\partial_{s}^{\alpha}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\beta}u(t)\in\tilde{H}^{d}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \rad_{s}(u(t))\geqslant\sigma t.

(1.10)

Consequently, the solution
u
of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) then is a strong solution of (
1.5
). Moreover, the pressure
p
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
.

Theorem
1.2.5
is the direct combination of Theorem
7.1.2
, which establishes the estimate of the radius of analyticity with respect to the vertical variable in (
1.10
), and Corollary
7.2.1
, which deduces from it the regularity with respect to the other variables. In fact, as we will see, the difficult point is to prove the smoothness of solutions in terms of global regularity, namely (
1.10
) with respect to the space variables.

It is surprising that the estimate of the radius of analyticity in the vertical direction obtained in Theorem
1.2.5
is better in large times than the one obtained in (
1.8
) for solutions of the incompressible Navier-Stokes system in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In fact, a linear estimate of the radius of analyticity in the context of the incompressible Navier-Stokes system on the torus, similar to the one in (
1.10
), was obtained in
[
20
]
, related to the fact that there is a spectral gap for the Laplace operator on the torus. In fact, in some sense, the estimate (
1.10
) also originates from some kind of spectral gap (see Proposition
2.4.8
), but not from the spectral gap of the Heisenberg sublaplacian, whose spectrum is
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Note that we do not claim any analyticity property of the solutions of (
1.5
) in the horizontal directions, and this is so far an open problem. This might come as a surprise in view of the dissipation law (
1.4
). In fact, the dissipation law (
1.4
) implies

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+4d\||D_{s}|^{1/2}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant 0,

see Proposition
2.4.8
. This is the key estimate to understand that the energy is dissipated in the direction of the commutators, and generates some analytic smoothing effect in the vertical direction.

1.3
Outline of the article

The article is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we describe some basic notions related to the Heisenberg group and several tools developed in
[
19
,
8
,
6
]
as the Fourier transform, which will sustain our strategy in this article. We finished this section by showing some useful results on the pseudo-differential operators, in the spirit of
[
7
]
, with the quantization given by the Fourier transform on the Heisenberg group. The main ideas and insights of the strategy are summarized in Section
3
. In Section
4
, we introduce Friedrichs multipliers, the Leray projector, and present their properties. They are used to study the nonstationary Stokes system and to get suitable approximate systems of (
1.5
). In Section
5
, we prove the global existence of weak solutions with finite energy for any initial data in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(Theorem
1.2.3
). Section
6
is devoted to establishing the global well-posedness of the solutions to (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) with small initial data in
\tilde{H}^{d}
(Theorem
1.2.4
). In Section
7
, we establish the smoothness of the solutions of (
1.5
) (Theorem
1.2.5
), first its analyticity with respect to the vertical variable
s
, and second its
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
smoothness with respect to all the variables. Section
8
discusses the existence of solutions to (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) with initial data belonging to
\dot{H}^{d}
and which are analytic with respect to the vertical variable.
Section
9
presents the derivation of sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes equations on a general stratified Lie group, and give an existence result of a weak solution in this setting in the energy space. This section ends up with some open problems. Finally, the appendices present several technical results.

Acknowledgments.

The author would like to thank Jean-Yves Chemin and Sylvain Ervedoza for their comments on this work and Hajer Bahouri, Jean-Yves Chemin and Raphaël Danchin for providing their manuscript
[
6
]
.

2
Heisenberg group

2.1
Lie group structure

Let
d
be a positive integer. The Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
is the set
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
endowed with the following group law

(Y,s)\cdot(Y^{\prime},s^{\prime}):=(Y+Y^{\prime},s+s^{\prime}+\langle\mathfrak{S}Y,Y^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}),

(2.1)

in which
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,
s
and
s^{\prime}
are in
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathfrak{S}
is the matrix introduced in (
1.1
). The group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
is a noncommutative Lie group, with
0
as the unit element and for which the inverse of an element
w\in\mathbb{H}
is given by
w^{-1}:=-w
. We denote the generic elements of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, as a couple
(Y,s)
, in which
Y=(y,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called the horizontal variable and
s\in\mathbb{R}
is called the vertical variable.

Lie algebra and the sub-Riemannian structure of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
.

The Lie algebra of left-invariant vector fields on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, denoted by
\mathfrak{h}^{d}
is the Lie algebra spanned by the following vector fields

P_{j}=X_{j}:=\partial_{y_{j}}+2\eta_{j}\partial_{s}\ \quad P_{j+d}=\Xi_{j}:=\partial_{\eta_{j}}-2y_{j}\partial_{s},\ \text{ with }j\in[\![1,d]\!],\ \text{ and }\ S:=-4\partial_{s}.

(2.2)

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}^{d}
has the following gradation

\mathfrak{h}^{d}=\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{d}\bigoplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{d}
, where
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{d}:=\text{Vect}(\{P_{j}\}_{j\in[\![1,2d]\!]})
and
\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{d}:=\text{Vect}(\{S\})
and we have the following identity

[X_{j},\Xi_{j}]=S,\quad\text{for any}\ j\in[\![1,d]\!],

(2.3)

in which
[A,B]:=AB-BA
denotes the commutator of the operators
A
and
B
. Accordingly, the Heisenberg group is a 2-step stratified Lie group, and is endowed with a sub-Riemannian structure (see
[
1
, Section 7.4, p. 209]
), where the space of horizontal vector fields is the first stratum, namely
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{d}
.

Note that the vector field
\partial_{s}
is a bi-invariant vector field and that we have the following identities

[X_{j},X_{k}]=[\Xi_{j},\Xi_{k}]=[X_{j},\partial_{s}]=[\Xi_{j},\partial_{s}]=0,\text{ and }[X_{j},\Xi_{k}]={\bf 1}_{j=k}S\quad\text{for all $j,k$ in $[\![1,d]\!]$}.

(2.4)

The Lie group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
is equipped with a bi-invariant Haar measure, which is simply the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
. We can write
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}
,
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}
, defined in (
1.2
), by using the family of left-invariant vector fields
(P_{j})_{j\in[\![1,2d]\!]}
as follows. For any smooth enough function
f
and horizontal vector field
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{2d})
on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, we have

\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}f=\!^{t}(P_{1}f,\dots,P_{2d}f),\ \ \ \divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=\sum_{j=1}^{2d}P_{j}u_{j}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}f=\sum_{j=1}^{2d}P_{j}^{2}f.

(2.5)

Note that the formula
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}=\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}\circ\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}
holds.

Let us finally emphasize that the family of vector fields
(P_{j})_{j\in[\![1,2d]\!]}
satisfies the Hörmander condition, and thus that the sub-Riemannian Laplacian
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
is hypoelliptic (see
[
27
]
).

In a similar way, we define the right-invariant vector fields
\tilde{P}_{j}=\tilde{X}_{j}
and
\tilde{P}_{j+d}=\tilde{\Xi}_{j}
with
j\in[\![1,d]\!]
as follows

\tilde{P}_{j}=\tilde{X}_{j}:=\partial_{y_{j}}-2\eta_{j}\partial_{s}\quad\text{ and }\quad\tilde{P}_{j+d}=\tilde{\Xi}_{j}:=\partial_{\eta_{j}}+2y_{j}\partial_{s}.

(2.6)

The Lie algebra
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{d}
of the right-invariant vector fields of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
is the Lie algebra generated by the family
(\tilde{P}_{j})_{j\in[\![1,2d]\!]}
. In view of the following formula

[\tilde{X}_{j},\tilde{\Xi}_{j}]=4\partial_{s},

with
j\in[\![1,d]\!]
, the Lie algebra
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{d}
is also
2
-step stratified. We define the right-invariant sub-Laplacian on
\mathbb{H}^{d}

\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}f:=\sum_{j=1}^{2d}\tilde{P}_{j}^{2}f.

(2.7)

The main interest of right-invariant vector fields is that they commute with the left-invariant vector fields:

[P_{i},\tilde{P}_{j}]=0,\quad\text{for any $i$ and $j$ in $[\![1,2d]\!]$}.

(2.8)

Notation 2.1.1
.

We will use the following notation: for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and for any multi-index
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{j}
,

P^{\alpha}:=P_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots P_{\alpha_{j}}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \tilde{P}^{\alpha}:=\tilde{P}_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\tilde{P}_{\alpha_{j}}.

(2.9)

Homogeneous Lie group structure of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
.

We now describe the homogeneous structure of the Heisenberg group, which provides properties for which the main tools of our analysis (scaling symmetry, Sobolev spaces associated with the sub-Laplacian, Fourier analysis and pseudo-differential operators on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
) are well developed in the literature (see for instance
[
19
,
7
,
5
,
6
]
). The homogeneous structure of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
is omnipresent in this article. The family of dilations
(\delta_{\mu})_{\mu>0}
on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
is defined for any
\mu>0
and
(Y,s)\in\mathbb{H}^{d}
, by

\delta_{\mu}(Y,s):=(\mu Y,\mu^{2}s).

Let us remark that for every
\mu>0
, for all smooth enough
f:\mathbb{H}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and for all
j\in[\![1,2d]\!]
, we have

P_{j}(f\circ\delta_{\mu})=\mu(P_{j}f)\circ\delta_{\mu},\ \ \tilde{P}_{j}(f\circ\delta_{\mu})=\mu(\tilde{P}_{j}f)\circ\delta_{\mu}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \partial_{s}(f\circ\delta_{\mu})=\mu^{2}(\partial_{s}f)\circ\delta_{\mu}.

(2.10)

This is, of course, reflected by the scaling invariance (
1.7
) for solutions of (
1.5
), given by
\mu\mapsto\mu u(\mu^{2}\cdot,\delta_{\mu}(\cdot))
.
For all
\mu>0
, the Jacobian of the dilation
\delta_{\mu}
is
\mu^{Q}
, where

Q:=2d+2

(2.11)

is called the
homogeneous dimension
of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
and for every
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
we have

\int_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}f(\delta_{\mu}(w))dw=\mu^{-Q}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}f(w)dw.

As we will see next (see Proposition
2.4.5
), the homogeneous dimension
Q
plays the same role in the exponent of Sobolev’s embedding for Sobolev spaces on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
as the algebraic dimension
2d+1
on the usual Sobolev embedding on
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
.

2.2
Fourier transform on the Heisenberg group

2.2.1
Definition of the Fourier transform

For every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
and
w=(y,\eta,s)\in\mathbb{H}^{d}
, we define the bounded operator
\mathrm{U}_{w}^{\lambda}
acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by setting

\mathrm{U}_{w}^{\lambda}u(x):=e^{-is\lambda-2i\lambda\langle\eta,x-y\rangle}u(x-2y),

(2.12)

for
u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
x
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For any
w\in\mathbb{H}^{d}
,
\mathrm{U}_{w}^{\lambda}
is a unitary operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
The family
(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda},L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}}

describes all the equivalence classes of the unitary dual of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
. According to the definition of the Fourier transform on locally compact Lie groups (see
[
19
]
), we define the Fourier transform of
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
evaluated at
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}
by the following formula

\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}):=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}f(w)\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}_{w}dw,\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}.

(2.13)

2.2.2
Frequency space approach

In order to get a precise description of the spectrum of
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
, we also use a description of the Fourier transform
\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
in terms of frequency space. This recent approach is developed in
[
7
,
8
,
6
]
, and gives an alternative definition of the Fourier transform on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, in which the Fourier modes are complex numbers and the Fourier transform of a function is a function defined on the following subset of
\mathbb{H}^{d}

\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}:=\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}.

For every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, we consider the orthonormal basis
(h_{n,\lambda})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
given by the rescaled Hermite functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, defined for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
by

h_{n,\lambda}:=|\lambda|^{\frac{d}{4}}h_{n}(|\lambda|^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot),

where

h_{n}:=\frac{1}{(2^{|n|}n!)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\prod_{j=1}^{d}(-\partial_{j}+x_{j})^{n_{j}}h_{0}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \ h_{0}:=\pi^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-\frac{|\cdot|^{2}}{2}}.

In particular, we have
\|h_{n,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=1
. According to (
2.13
), for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, the Fourier mode
\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})
of a function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
is completely determined by the following family of complex numbers
(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda))_{(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{2d}}
given by

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda):=\langle\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})h_{m,\lambda},h_{n,\lambda}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\quad\text{ for }(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{2d}.

(2.14)

For every
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we thus define the function
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f):(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda)\in\mathbb{C}
. This function
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)
provides a suitable alternative definition of the Fourier transform of
f
, sought as a complex valued function defined on
\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
.

Now, we recall classical properties of this Fourier transform that can be found in
[
19
,
8
,
7
]
. We define the measure
d\widehat{w}
in
\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
by setting, for any measurable functions
f:\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
,

\int_{\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}}f(\widehat{w})d\widehat{w}:=\sum_{(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{*}}f(n,m,\lambda)|\lambda|^{d}d\lambda.

(2.15)

The measure
|\lambda|^{d}d\lambda
corresponds, up to multiplication by a constant, to the classical Plancherel measure on the Heisenberg group (see
[
19
, Example 1.8.4, p. 45]
). Let us point out that the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and its dual
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
respectively coincide with
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
.

By using the Plancherel theorem and inversion theorem for
\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
(see
[
19
, Theorem 1.8.5 and Corollary 1.8.6, p. 46]
), we can deduce the corresponding theorem for the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
(see
[
7
, Theorem 1.3]
and
[
6
]
for an elementary and self-contained approach):

Proposition 2.2.1
.

If
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, then for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, the operator
\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})
is a trace class (thus Hilbert-Schmidt) operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and for any
w\in\mathbb{H}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle f(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{d-1}}{\pi^{d+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr\left(\mathrm{U}_{w^{-1}}^{\lambda}\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\right)|\lambda|^{d}d\lambda.

Moreover,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
can be extended as a bi-continuous isomorphism from
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
L^{2}(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d})
, and then for every
f
and
g
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we get

\langle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f),\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(g)\rangle_{L^{2}(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr\left(\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(g)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})^{*}\right)|\lambda|^{d}d\lambda=\frac{\pi^{d+1}}{2^{d-1}}\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})},

where
L^{2}(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}):=L^{2}(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d},d\widehat{w})
and the measure
d\widehat{w}
is defined in (
2.15
).

2.3
Preliminary results on the pseudo-differential calculus on
\mathbb{H}^{d}

The most useful interest of the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
in this article is that (see
[
7
, p.6]
), for any
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}f)(n,m,\lambda)=4|\lambda|(2|m|+d)\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda),

(2.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}f)(n,m,\lambda)=4|\lambda|(2|n|+d)\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda).

(2.17)

These two formulas are the analog of the fact that the symbol of the Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is exactly
-|\xi|^{2}
. Following this analogy, the operators
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
are Fourier multipliers with respect to the quantization on the Lie group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
.

In this paper, we will use the symbols of the powers of
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
with respect to the Fourier transform on the Heisenberg group. In this subsection, we begin by introducing the definition of the powers of the sub-Laplacian as unbounded operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and then give their symbols with respect to the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
. The case of the negative powers requires more work (see Section
B
, Lemma
B.0.3
) in order to identify their symbols on the intersection of their domains and the Schwartz classes. We will also give the expression of
P_{j}
,
\tilde{P}_{j}
and
\partial_{s}
, which are pseudo-differential operators, with respect to the Fourier transform on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
.

Power of the sub-Laplacians.

In order to define the fractional powers of the Heisenberg sub-Laplacians on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we use the functional calculus for
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
. The operator
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
is a positive self-adjoint unbounded operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
with
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\subset\mathrm{Dom}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})
. According to the spectral theorem, denoting by
E
the spectral measure of
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
, for any measurable function
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we can define an unbounded operator
\psi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, with domain

\mathrm{Dom}(\psi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})):=\left\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})~\middle|~\int_{0}^{+\infty}|\psi(\mu)|^{2}d\langle E(\mu)f,f\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})}<+\infty\right\},

by the formula

\psi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}):=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\psi(\mu)dE(\mu).

(2.18)

Similarly, for
\tilde{E}
the spectral measure of
-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
, for any measurable function
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we set

\psi(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}):=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\psi(\mu)d\tilde{E}(\mu),

(2.19)

with

\mathrm{Dom}(\psi(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})):=\left\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})~\middle|~\int_{0}^{+\infty}|\psi(\mu)|^{2}d\langle\tilde{E}(\mu)f,f\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})}<+\infty\right\}.

Furthermore, if
\ell>-Q/4
, the space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
is contained in
\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell})
and
\mathrm{Dom}((-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell})
(see
[
19
, Proposition 4.4.13, Item 2, p. 230]
).

Inspired by (
2.17
) and (
2.16
), we give the following representation formula by using the Fourier transform on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
.

Proposition 2.3.1
.

Let
\ell^{\prime}>0
,
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, and
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. We have, for
(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}((\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}f)(n,m,\lambda)=(1+4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}((\Id-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}f)(n,m,\lambda)=(1+4|\lambda|(2|n|+d))^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\partial_{s}f)(n,m,\lambda)=i\lambda\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(|D_{s}|^{\ell^{\prime}}f)(n,m,\lambda)=|\lambda|^{\ell^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda).

If
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell})
, we have, for
(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
,

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}f)(n,m,\lambda)=(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda),

and if
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell})
, we have, for
(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
,

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}((-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}f)(n,m,\lambda)=(4|\lambda|(2|n|+d))^{\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda).

Remark 2.3.2
.

To our knowledge, the action of the Fourier transform
\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
on the sub-Laplacians
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
is known in the literature. However, this is to our knowledge the first explicit mention of how the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
acts simultaneously on the powers of both sub-Laplacians
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
, especially for negative powers
\ell
.

Proof.

For
(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}
and
(\Id-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}
, this follows from Proposition
B.0.1
and the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
. The formula for
\partial_{s}
and
|D_{s}|^{\ell^{\prime}}
follows immediately from the formula (see for instance
[
7
, Equation (1.9)]
)

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}e^{-is\lambda}W(n,m,\lambda,Y)f(Y,s)dYds,

(2.20)

for
(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
, where, writing
Y=(y,\eta)
with
y
and
\eta
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
W
is defined by

W(n,m,\lambda,Y):=e^{is\lambda}\langle\mathrm{U}_{(Y,s)}^{\lambda}h_{m,\lambda},h_{n,\lambda}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-2i\lambda\langle\eta,x-y\rangle}h_{m,\lambda}(x-2y)h_{n,\lambda}(x)dx,

(2.21)

which thus does not depend on the variable
s
.
For
(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}
and
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}
, the formula follows from Proposition
B.0.1
(for
\ell\geqslant 0
) and Lemma
B.0.3
(for
\ell<0
) combined with the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
.
∎

Let us now describe the action of the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
on the left-invariant and the right-invariant operators in the following proposition (see for instance
[
7
, Proposition A.3]
).

Proposition 2.3.3
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Then, for any
j\in[\![1,d]\!]
, we have

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(X_{j}f)=-\mathcal{M}^{+}_{j}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)\text{ and }\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Xi_{j}f)=-\mathcal{M}^{-}_{j}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f),

(2.22)

where, for any
(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
,

\mathcal{M}^{+}_{j}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda)=\sqrt{2|\lambda|}\begin{cases}\sqrt{m_{j}+1}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m+e_{j},\lambda)-\sqrt{m_{j}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m-e_{j},\lambda)\text{ if }m_{j}\neq 0,\\
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m+e_{j},\lambda)\text{ if }m_{j}=0,\\
\end{cases}

and

\mathcal{M}^{-}_{j}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda)=i\frac{\sqrt{2}\lambda}{\sqrt{|\lambda|}}\begin{cases}\sqrt{m_{j}+1}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m+e_{j},\lambda)+\sqrt{m_{j}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m-e_{j},\lambda)\text{ if }m_{j}\neq 0,\\
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m+e_{j},\lambda)\text{ if }m_{j}=0,\\
\end{cases}

where
e_{j}=(e^{k}_{j})_{k\in[\![1,d]\!]}
belongs to
\mathbb{N}^{d}
and satisfies
e^{k}_{j}=1
if
k=j
and
e^{k}_{j}=0
if
k\neq j
. Similarly, for any
j\in[\![1,d]\!]
, we have

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\tilde{X}_{j}f)=\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{+}_{j}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)\text{ and }\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\tilde{\Xi}_{j}f)=\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{-}_{j}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f),

(2.23)

where, for any
(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
,

\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{+}_{j}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda)=\sqrt{2|\lambda|}\begin{cases}\sqrt{n_{j}+1}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n+e_{j},m,\lambda)-\sqrt{n_{j}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n-e_{j},m,\lambda)\text{ if }n_{j}\neq 0,\\
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n+e_{j},m,\lambda)\text{ if }n_{j}=0,\\
\end{cases}

and

\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{-}_{j}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda)=i\frac{\sqrt{2}\lambda}{\sqrt{|\lambda|}}\begin{cases}\sqrt{n_{j}+1}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n+e_{j},m,\lambda)+\sqrt{n_{j}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n-e_{j},m,\lambda)\text{ if }n_{j}\neq 0,\\
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n+e_{j},m,\lambda)\text{ if }n_{j}=0.\\
\end{cases}

Proof.

For the left-invariant vector fields, the formulas can be found in
[
7
, Proposition A.3]
. In order to show the formulas for the right-invariant vector fields, we will use another approach, which allows to establish the case of the left-invariant vector fields and the right-invariant vector fields simultaneously using the pseudo-differential calculus on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
. Let us recall that we saw in the proof of Proposition
B.0.1
that, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
,
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(X_{j})=-2\partial_{x_{j}}
and
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(\Xi_{j})=-2i\lambda x_{j}
.

Besides, for any
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we have

\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(X_{j}f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(X_{j})\text{ and }\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\tilde{X}_{j}f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(X_{j})\circ\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}),

see
[
19
, Proposition 1.7.6, p. 40]
.

Then we can recover the formula for
X_{j}
and
\Xi_{j}
from the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}
(see (
2.14
)) and the
following classical recurrence relation for the Hermite functions: for any
j\in[\![1,d]\!]
and
m=(m_{1},\dots,m_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
m_{j}\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{j}h_{m,\lambda}=\frac{|\lambda|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}\left(\sqrt{2m_{j}}h_{m-e_{j},\lambda}-\sqrt{2m_{j}+2}h_{m+e_{j},\lambda}\right),

\displaystyle x_{j}h_{m,\lambda}=\frac{1}{2|\lambda|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\sqrt{2m_{j}}h_{m-e_{j},\lambda}+\sqrt{2m_{j}+2}h_{m+e_{j},\lambda}\right).

The formulas for
\tilde{X}_{j}
and
\tilde{\Xi}_{j}
follow similarly.
∎

Let us note that the maps
\mathcal{M}^{\pm}_{j}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\pm}_{j}
, with
j\in[\![1,d]\!]
, are not multiplicative operators. This reflects the fact that
X_{j}
is not a Fourier multiplier on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
. In fact, it is a pseudo-differential operator with respect to the quantization on the Lie group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
.

2.4
Sobolev spaces

Let us now introduce the Sobolev-type spaces associated with
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
. In particular, all the results on these Sobolev spaces in this article are listed in
[
19
, Theorem 4.4.28, p. 246 and Theorem 4.4.29, p. 248]
. For the definitions of Sobolev spaces, we refer to
[
19
, Definition 4.4.2, p. 219]
in the inhomogeneous case and
[
19
, Definition 4.4.12, p. 230]
in the homogeneous case. The fractional powers of the sub-Laplacians can be defined in the
L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
framework by an abstract way (see
[
19
, Theorem 4.3.6, p. 203]
).
In the case of
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, this definition coincides with the definition that uses the functional calculus, see (
2.18
) and (
2.19
), with
\psi(\mu)=\mu^{\ell}
where
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let
p\in(1,+\infty)
and
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
.

1.

The (inhomogeneous) Sobolev space
W^{\ell,p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
is the set of tempered distributions obtained by the completion of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
with respect to the norm given for any
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
by

\|f\|_{W_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,p}}:=\|(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}f\|_{L^{p}}.

2.

We define the (homogeneous) Sobolev space
\dot{W}^{\ell,p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
as the set of tempered distributions obtained by the completion of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})_{p}^{\frac{\ell}{2}})
with respect to the norm given for any
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})_{p}^{\frac{\ell}{2}})
by

\|f\|_{\dot{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,p}}:=\|(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}f\|_{L^{p}},

where
\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})_{p}^{\frac{\ell}{2}})
is the domain of
(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(see
[
19
, Theorem 4.3.6, p. 203]
).

3.

If
p=2
, we set

H^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d}):=W^{\ell,2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\ \text{ and }\ \dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d}):=\dot{W}^{\ell,2}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d}),

which are endowed respectively with the following scalar products

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H^{\ell}}:=\langle(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot,(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}\ \text{ and }\ \langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\dot{H}^{\ell}}:=\langle(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot,(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}.

By definition, the spaces
W_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,p}
and
\dot{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,p}
are Banach spaces and
H^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
are Hilbert spaces. Let us note that for all
p\in(1,+\infty)
, if
\ell>-Q/p
, then
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\subset\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell/2}_{p})
(see
[
19
, Proposition 4.4.13, Item 2, p. 230]
), so that
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})_{p}^{{\ell}/{2}})=\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

The following proposition is continuously used in this article.

Proposition 2.4.2

(
[
19
, Theorem 4.4.16, p. 233]
)
.

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
,
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in(1,+\infty)
, the operators
P^{\alpha}
(recall the notation (
2.9
)) are bounded from
W_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell^{\prime},p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
W_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell^{\prime}-\ell,p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and from
\dot{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell^{\prime},p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
\dot{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell^{\prime}-\ell,p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

In the following, we will use the regularity properties corresponding to the left-invariant sub-Laplacian
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and to the right-invariant sub-Laplacian
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
. Therefore, since the Sobolev spaces
H^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
measure only the regularity with respect to
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
, we also introduce the following Sobolev spaces corresponding to the regularity properties with respect to
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
.

Definition 2.4.3
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in(1,+\infty)
. We define the Sobolev space
\tilde{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
as the subspace of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
obtained as the completion of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{p}^{\frac{\ell}{2}})
for the norm defined for any
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{p}^{\frac{\ell}{2}})
by

\|f\|_{\tilde{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,p}}:=\|(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}f\|_{L^{p}},

where
\mathrm{Dom}((-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}_{p})
denotes the domain of
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(see
[
19
, Theorem 4.3.6, p. 203]
). We also set

\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d}):=\tilde{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,2}(\mathbb{H}^{d}),

which we equip with the following scalar product

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{\ell}}:=\langle(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot,(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Accordingly, the space
\tilde{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
is a Banach space and
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
is a Hilbert space.

Moreover, using the formulas of Proposition
2.3.1
, we deduce the following homogeneity properties for the homogeneous Sobolev norms: for any
\mu>0
and
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\forall u\in\dot{H}^{\ell},\quad\|u\circ\delta_{\mu}\|_{\dot{H}^{\ell}}=\mu^{\ell-Q/2}\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{\ell}}\ \text{ and }\ \forall v\in\tilde{H}^{\ell},\quad\|v\circ\delta_{\mu}\|_{\tilde{H}^{\ell}}=\mu^{\ell-Q/2}\|v\|_{\tilde{H}^{\ell}}.

(2.24)

Remark 2.4.4
.

The Sobolev spaces
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
are not comparable in general. In particular, for
d=1
one can find a function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
X_{1}f
and
\Xi_{1}f
belong to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{1})
and
\tilde{X}_{1}f\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{1})
(see
[
19
, Example 4.4.32, p. 250]
).

We have the following properties (see
[
19
, Theorem 4.4.28, p. 246]
).

Proposition 2.4.5
.

1.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in(1,+\infty)
. Then we have the following norm equivalence

\|\cdot\|_{\dot{W}^{\ell,p}_{\mathbb{H}}}\sim\sum_{\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}}\|P^{\alpha}\cdot\|_{L^{p}},

where the operators
P^{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
are defined in (
2.9
).

2.

The space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
is dense in
W^{\ell,p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and in
\dot{W}^{\ell,p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
if
\ell>-Q/p
and
p\in(1,+\infty)
.

3.

If
1<p<q<+\infty
, and
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
are real numbers satisfying
\ell^{\prime}-\ell=Q(1/p-1/q)
, then
\dot{W}^{\ell^{\prime},p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow\dot{W}^{\ell,q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
W^{\ell^{\prime},p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{\ell,q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Moreover, if
p\in(1,+\infty)
and
\ell>Q/p
, then
W_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

4.

If
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
,
p
and
q
belong to
(1,+\infty)
and satisfy
1/p+1/q=1
, then the dual of
W^{\ell,p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
is
W^{-\ell,q}_{\mathbb{H}}
and the dual of
\dot{W}^{\ell,p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
is
\dot{W}^{-\ell,q}_{\mathbb{H}}
.

Remark 2.4.6
.

If we replace
\dot{W}^{\ell,p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
by
\tilde{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
,
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
by
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
P_{j}
by
\tilde{P}_{j}
, then Proposition
2.4.5
and
2.4.7
holds for
\tilde{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell,p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. It follows from Proposition
2.4.2

that for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
and
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, the operators
\tilde{P}^{\alpha}
are bounded from
\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}-\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

The following results concern the product estimates (see
[
6
]
).

Proposition 2.4.7
.

If
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
belong to
(-Q/2,Q/2)
are such that
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}>0
, there is a constant
C_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
such that for any
f\in\dot{H}^{\ell_{1}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
g\in\dot{H}^{\ell_{2}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, the product
fg
belongs to
\dot{H}^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-Q/2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and

\|fg\|_{\dot{H}^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-Q/2}}\leqslant C_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{\ell_{1}}}\|g\|_{\dot{H}^{\ell_{2}}}.

(2.25)

Let us finish with the following proposition which is crucial in this article.

Proposition 2.4.8
.

For
\ell\geqslant 0
, for every
f\in\dot{H}^{2\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(respectively
f\in\tilde{H}^{2\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
), we have

\||D_{s}|^{\ell}f\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{(4d)^{\ell}}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{2\ell}}\ \ \ \left(\text{respectively }\ \||D_{s}|^{\ell}f\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{(4d)^{\ell}}\|f\|_{\tilde{H}^{2\ell}}\right),

where
|D_{s}|^{\ell}
is the Fourier multiplier on
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
of symbol
|\xi_{2d+1}|^{\ell}
.

Proof.

The proof is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.3.1
, since
4|\lambda|(2|m|+d)\geqslant 4d|\lambda|
and
4|\lambda|(2|n|+d)\geqslant 4d|\lambda|
respectively.
∎

Note that according to the homogeneity properties of
|D_{s}|
with respect to the dilation
\delta
and of the homogeneous Sobolev norms (see (
2.24
)), the Sobolev indexes in Proposition
2.4.8
are optimal.

2.5
Order of commutators on
\mathbb{H}^{d}

The first difficulty that appears in this article is the following:
the commutator of two pseudo-differential operators on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
is in general the sum of the orders of the two pseudo-differential operators
. In particular, we cannot expect to gain in regularity by estimating the commutator of two pseudo-differential operators, compared to the estimate of the product (in the Sobolev scales
{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and / or
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
). In this text we refer to the order of an operator on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
with respect to a sub-Laplacian, defined as follows:

Definition 2.5.1
.

Let
\ell
be a real number and
\mathrm{T}:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. We say that
\mathrm{T}
is of an operator of order
\ell
with respect to the left-invariant sub-Laplacian (respectively to the right-invariant sub-Laplacian) if

1.

\mathrm{T}
is homogeneous of degree
\ell
:
\mathrm{T}(\varphi\circ\delta_{\mu})=\mu^{\ell}(\mathrm{T}\varphi)\circ\delta_{\mu}
for any
\mu>0
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
;

2.

the operators
\mathrm{T}\circ(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}
and
(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}\circ\mathrm{T}
(respectively
\mathrm{T}\circ(\Id-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}
and
(\Id-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}\circ\mathrm{T}
) belong to
\mathcal{L}(L^{2})
.

If
h
and
h^{\prime}
are two positive integers and
\mathrm{T}:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})^{h}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})^{h^{\prime}}
is a bounded operator, then the same terminology occurs with the obvious modifications on the definition.

It follows from Proposition
2.4.8
that
|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\partial_{s}=-\frac{1}{4}[X_{i},\Xi_{i}]=\frac{1}{4}[\tilde{X}_{j},\tilde{\Xi}_{j}]
for
i
and
j
in
[\![1,d]\!]
are respectively operators of order
1
and
2
with respect to both the left-invariant sub-Laplacian and the right-invariant sub-Laplacian.

Note that, using the homogeneity of the item
1
of Definition
2.5.1
, the commutator of two operators
\mathrm{T}_{1}
of order
\ell_{1}
and
\mathrm{T}_{2}
of order
\ell_{2}
is of order
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
except if
[\mathrm{T}_{1},\mathrm{T}_{2}]=0
, i.e. when
\mathrm{T}_{1}
and
\mathrm{T}_{2}
commute.

3
Main ideas

The main consequence of the noncommutativity properties of operators
P_{j}
(see (
2.3
)) in the sub-Riemannian Navier-Stokes system on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
is that the computation of the pressure from the equation (
1.5
) involves a term depending linearly on
u
, so that
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}p
involves an operator of order
2
in
u
and consequently is part of the diffusive operator.

Pressure and loss of derivatives.

Let us explain this more precisely. In order to compute formally the pressure, we apply the divergence on the first line of (
1.5
). According to the free divergence condition, it follows that

p=(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-1}\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u)+(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-1}\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u).

(3.1)

We then define the horizontal Leray projector
\mathbb{P}
on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
by

\mathbb{P}:=\Id+\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-1}\circ\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}.

(3.2)

Thus System (
1.5
) is formally equivalent to

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u+(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u+\mathbb{P}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u)=0\ &\text{in}\ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\\
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0\ &\text{in}\ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\end{cases}

since

-\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}p=(\Id-\mathbb{P})\left(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\right).

The difficulty here comes from the term

(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u,

which is of order
2
in
u
with respect to the left-invariant sub-Laplacian (see Definition
2.5.1
), even when we restrict the operator to the space of divergence free vector fields, see Lemma
4.2.3
.

Indeed, when
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0
, we obviously have
(\Id-\mathbb{P})u=0
, so one might expect that

(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u=[(\Id-\mathbb{P}),\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}]u

would provide some gain of regularity (which is the usual property of commutators when considering the usual Sobolev scales
H^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
, but these spaces are not adapted to our case since
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
is not elliptic). As we mentioned in Subsection
2.5
, this is not the case in general when considering pseudo-differential operators on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, and we will indeed prove in Lemma
4.2.3
that there is no gain there for the operators
(\Id-\mathbb{P})
and
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
.
1
1

1

The presence of the above term contrasts with the system introduced in
[
33
]
and the Navier-Stokes system on the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, where the corresponding Leray projector commutes with the diffusive operator.

L^{2}
energy estimates and weak solutions.

Despite this fact, the pressure term can be canceled using the following property: for
u
satisfying
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0
,

\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}p,u\rangle_{L^{2}}=-\langle p,\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,

(3.3)

which is valid if
u
is sufficiently smooth. Note that this is the cancellation that appears in the derivation of the dissipation law of the kinetic energy (
1.4
). This is useful when dealing with weak solutions lying in the energy space as in Definition
1.2.2
and will be of primary importance in the proof of Theorem
1.2.3
.

Critical framework and right-invariant vector fields.

But this
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
space is not scaling invariant for (
1.5
), in the sense that the transform (
1.7
) does not preserve the
L^{\infty}(L^{2})
-norm. A natural critical (scaling invariant) space for (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) would be the space
\dot{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
,
but we cannot cancel the pressure term as in (
3.3
) for the
\dot{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
scalar product because
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}
does not commute with left-invariant operators
P_{i}
with
i\in[\![1,2d]\!]
. Indeed, in this space the computation of the energy corresponding to the linear part of (
1.5
) yields

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}+\langle(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u,u\rangle_{\dot{H}^{d}}.

Yet, the third term
\langle(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}}
has no sign and is of main order. Thus if this term is too large, the dissipation fails.
Nevertheless, if
\mathrm{T}
is an operator that commutes with all vector fields
P_{i}
with
i\in[\![1,2d]\!]
, we have

\langle\mathrm{T}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}p,\mathrm{T}u\rangle_{L^{2}}=-\langle\mathrm{T}p,\mathrm{T}\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)\rangle_{L^{2}}=0.

This is the main strategy that we use to make the energy methods work for existence results. In this article, we will in particular use
\mathrm{T}:=(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}
, which will eventually provide the existence part of Theorem
1.2.4
. In a nutshell, the regularity with respect to the right-invariant vector fields is propagated in the energy estimate.

Loss of derivatives in nonlinearity and right-invariant vector fields.

In this paper, this choice is also used in order to gain one derivative in the commutator estimates involving the convection operator
u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}
. Let us explain this in more detail in the case
d=1
. Let
u
and
v
be two smooth enough horizontal vector fields on
\mathbb{H}^{1}
. If
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}\in\{\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{\Xi}_{1}\}
, then, since
[\tilde{\mathcal{Z}},\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]=0
, we have

[\tilde{\mathcal{Z}},u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]v=\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v,

(3.4)

while, if
\mathcal{Z}\in\{X_{1},\Xi_{1}\}
, we have

[\mathcal{Z},u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]v=\mathcal{Z}u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v+u\cdot[\mathcal{Z},\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]v.

(3.5)

Since
[\mathcal{Z},\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]
is of order
2
(see Proposition
2.4.8
), we lose one derivative in (
3.5
) compared to (
3.4
).
This is the main point in the proof of the stability part of Theorem
1.2.4
.

Vertical smoothing effects.

The crucial idea to obtain the smoothing effects in the vertical direction is to use the dissipation of the energy for the sub-elliptic heat equation on the Heisenberg group. Let us explain this. Let
u\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{2})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
be a smooth enough (so that the following calculus makes sense) solution of

\partial_{t}u-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u=0\ \ \ \ \text{ in }(0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d}.

We have

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=0.

Let
\sigma>0
. If we set
U(t):=e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u(t)
for any
t>0
, then

\partial_{t}U-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}U=\sigma|D_{s}|U\ \ \ \ \text{ in }(0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d}.

In view of Proposition
2.4.8
, we have

\sigma\langle|D_{s}|U,U\rangle_{L^{2}}=\sigma\||D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}U\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{\sigma}{4d}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}U\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

It follows that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|U\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{4d}\right)\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}U\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leqslant 0.

(3.6)

Accordingly, for
\sigma<4d
, the function
t\mapsto\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
is decreasing and we get

\forall t>0,\ \ \ \|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant\|u(0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

that is,
u(t)
is analytic with respect to the variable
s
for any
t>0
. This is the underlying idea for the proof of the analytic regularity in the vertical variable stated in Theorem
1.2.5
.

Horizontal smoothing effects.

In this paragraph, we give the flavor of the arguments to derive estimates on the solution of (
1.5
) in
\dot{H}^{k}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
from the vertical analytic regularizing properties derived above. We do that with
k=1
, as the general case follows similarly.

Let
u
be a smooth solution of

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u+(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u=0\ &\text{in}\ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\\
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0\ &\text{in}\ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\\
u_{|_{t=0}}=u_{0}\ &\text{in}\ \mathbb{H}^{d},\end{cases}

with
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Let
\sigma\in(0,4d)
. Then, similarly as in (
3.6
), we can obtain

\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u\|_{L^{2}(L^{2})}\leqslant\frac{\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\min\{1,2-\sigma/(2d)\}}.

Then there exists
t_{0}>0
such that
u(t_{0})\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Thus, we have

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}\leqslant|\langle(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})u,u\rangle_{\dot{H}^{1}}|\ \ \text{on }(t_{0},+\infty).

This implies

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}((t_{0},+\infty);\dot{H}^{1})}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u

\displaystyle\|_{L^{2}((t_{0},+\infty);\dot{H}^{1})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\|u(t_{0})\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}+\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}|\langle(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})u,u\rangle_{\dot{H}^{1}}dt.

(3.7)

The main point now is the following identity

(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})u=\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{s}u,

proved in Lemma
4.2.3
, with
\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{1})
commuting with
\partial_{s}
. Then, we have

\forall t>0,\ \ |\langle(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})u,u\rangle_{\dot{H}^{1}}|\leqslant\|\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{1})}\||D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}.

Since
t_{0}>0
and
e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u\in L^{2}(\dot{H}^{1})
, we have
|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u\in L^{2}((t_{0},+\infty);\dot{H}^{1})
. Thus, the right-hand side of (
3.7
) is finite and we deduce that
u\in L^{\infty}((t_{0},+\infty);\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))\cap L^{2}((t_{0},+\infty);\dot{H}^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
.

4
Derivation of a suitable approximate system

4.1
Homogeneous Friedrichs multipliers on
\mathbb{H}^{d}

In this section we define an approximate version of System (
1.5
) by performing a Friedrichs-type method in our context.

In the context of the Navier-Stokes system on the Euclidean setting, the Friedrichs method aims to construct approximate systems of the original system by truncation in the spectrum of the Stokes operators or using the Fourier transform in the case of the torus or the whole space, which provides a more flexible procedure in the last two cases. Due to the hypoellipticity of
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
, the strategy that consists of using a spectral decomposition associated with the Stokes operators appears to be trickier than in the Euclidean setting. We choose to use a Fourier analysis approach based on the Fourier transform on the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
. This approach provides a highly flexible and unified framework to manipulate all the regularity and differential operators that appear in our strategy.

In order to work on the
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
framework for System (
1.5
), our analysis must involve two different regularities, with respect to the space variable
(Y,s)
, that are: the required regularity in order to give a “strong sense” of all terms of System (
1.5
), which is
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
with
\ell\geqslant 2
, and the required regularity in order to work on the Sobolev-type scale
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
with
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
. The most conceptual obstruction of this idea is that these two regularities (the regularity given by
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
) do not coincide in general (see Remark
2.4.4
). In order to overcome this difficulty, we construct spectral multipliers that regularize simultaneously with respect to the
H^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
regularity and that commutes with the two sub-Laplacians
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
. This is possible by taking advantage of the Fourier transform on the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
as follows. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the
bi-stratified Friedrichs multiplier
\mathsf{J}_{k}
by setting for all smooth enough complex value function
f
on
\mathbb{H}^{d}

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathsf{J}_{k}f)(n,m,\lambda):=\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant 4|\lambda|(2|m|+d)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant 4|\lambda|(2|n|+d)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda),

with
(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
. We also define the Friedrichs multiplier
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
associated with the right-invariant Laplacian
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
by setting

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}f)(n,m,\lambda):=\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant 4|\lambda|(2|n|+d)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda).

Remark 4.1.1
.

Let us point out that we cannot expect the
\tilde{H}^{\ell}
regularity if we localize in Fourier space only by using
\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant 4|\lambda|(2|m|+d)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}
since the
\dot{H}^{\ell}
and the
\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}}
regularities are not comparable in general (see
[
19
, Example 4.4.32, p. 250]
) as explained in Remark
2.4.4
. This fact can be seen by using the Fourier transform on the Heisenberg group according to Proposition
2.3.1
: among the parameters
(n,m,\lambda)\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}
, the action of a left-invariant operator involves only
(m,\lambda)
while the action of a right-invariant operator involves only
(n,\lambda)
.

Notation 4.1.2
.

If
E
and
F
are two topological vector spaces, we denote by
\mathcal{L}(E,F)
the space of continuous linear maps from
E
to
F
and we denote
\mathcal{L}(E):=\mathcal{L}(E,E)
.

The following proposition summarizes the useful properties of the bi-stratified Friedrichs multipliers
\mathsf{J}_{k}
.

Proposition 4.1.3
.

Let
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
two real numbers and
k
a nonnegative integer. Then

1.

\mathsf{J}_{k}
belongs to
