\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\ell},\dot{H}^{\ell^{\prime}})\cap\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{\ell},\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}})
, and we have

\|\mathsf{J}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\ell},\dot{H}^{\ell^{\prime}})}\leqslant 2^{\frac{(k+1)|\ell-\ell^{\prime}|}{2}}\ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \|\mathsf{J}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{\ell},\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}})}\leqslant 2^{\frac{(k+1)|\ell-\ell^{\prime}|}{2}},

2.

\mathsf{J}_{k}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\ell},\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}})\cap\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{\ell},\dot{H}^{\ell^{\prime}})
, and we have

\|\mathsf{J}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\ell},\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}})}\leqslant 2^{\frac{(k+1)(|\ell|+|\ell^{\prime}|)}{2}}\ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \|\mathsf{J}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{\ell},\dot{H}^{\ell^{\prime}})}\leqslant 2^{\frac{(k+1)(|\ell|+|\ell^{\prime}|)}{2}},

3.

\left[\mathsf{J}_{k},(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}\right]=\left[\mathsf{J}_{k},(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}\right]=\left[\mathsf{J}_{k},(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}\right]=\left[\mathsf{J}_{k},(\Id-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}\right]=0,

4.

for any
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\left[\mathsf{J}_{k},e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\right]=\left[\mathsf{J}_{k},|D_{s}|^{\ell}\right]=\left[\mathsf{J}_{k},\partial_{s}\right]=0,

5.

\mathsf{J}_{k}
is a bounded self-adjoint operator on
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and on
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
,

6.

if
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, then for any
f\in\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(respectively
f\in\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
), the sequence
(\mathsf{J}_{k}f)
converges to
f
in
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(respectively in
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
),

7.

\mathsf{J}_{k}^{2}=\mathsf{J}_{k}
.

We emphasize that, in view of the first point, we can control the norm of
\mathsf{J}_{k}
in
\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{\ell})
or in
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{\ell})
, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
independently of
k\in\mathbb{N}
, that is
\|\mathsf{J}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{\ell})}\leqslant 1
and
\|\mathsf{J}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{\ell})}\leqslant 1
.

Proof.

We give only the proof of the first two points. The proofs of the other points are left to the reader, and follow from Lemma
2.3.1
, the Plancherel formula on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
and the definition of
\mathsf{J}_{k}
. For the first and the second points, we prove only the first inequality because the proof of the second ones are similar.

Let
f
be in
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. According to the Plancherel formula on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{J}_{k}f\|_{\dot{H}^{\ell}}^{2}=\frac{2^{d-1}}{\pi^{d+1}}\!\sum_{(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{\ell}\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant 4|\lambda|(2|m|+d)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 165.02606pt\times\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant 4|\lambda|(2|n|+d)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda)|^{2}|\lambda|^{d}d\lambda.

(4.1)

1.
For any
(n,m,\lambda)\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}
, we have

(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{\ell^{\prime}-\ell}\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant 4|\lambda|(2|m|+d)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}\leqslant(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{\ell^{\prime}}2^{(k+1)|\ell-\ell^{\prime}|}.

Thus, by bounding the indicator function of the set
\{2^{-(k+1)}\leqslant 4|\lambda|(2|n|+d)\leqslant 2^{k}\}
by
1
, we deduce from (
4.1
) and the Plancherel formula that

\|\mathsf{J}_{k}f\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\ell}}\leqslant 2^{(k+1)|\ell-\ell^{\prime}|}\|f\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\ell^{\prime}}}.

2.
Let
(n,m,\lambda)\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}
. Separating the cases
\ell\geqslant 0
and
\ell<0
, we get

(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{\ell}\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant 4|\lambda|(2|m|+d)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}\leqslant 2^{(k+1)|\ell|}.

(4.2)

Besides, also by reasoning on the sign of
\ell^{\prime}
, we obtain

\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant 4|\lambda|(2|n|+d)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}\leqslant 2^{(k+1)|\ell^{\prime}|}(4|\lambda|(2|n|+d))^{\ell^{\prime}}.

(4.3)

Then, thanks to (
4.2
) and (
4.3
), it follows from (
4.1
) and the Plancherel formula on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
that

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{J}_{k}f\|_{\dot{H}^{\ell}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{(k+1)(|\ell|+|\ell^{\prime}|)}\frac{2^{d-1}}{\pi^{d+1}}\sum_{(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(4|\lambda|(2|n|+d))^{\ell^{\prime}}|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda)|^{2}|\lambda|^{d}d\lambda

\displaystyle=2^{(k+1)(|\ell|+|\ell^{\prime}|)}\|f\|_{\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}}}^{2}.

∎

The operators
\mathsf{J}_{k}
regularize with respect to the regularities generated by the left-invariant and the right-invariant fields (see the first two points of Proposition
4.1.3
). Unfortunately, they do not commute with
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}
and then we cannot propagate
\mathsf{J}_{k}
in (
1.5
). However, the Fourier multiplier
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
commutes with
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}
and can thus be propagated in (
1.5
). We now give the properties of
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.1.4
.

Let
k
be a nonnegative integer. Then

1.

for any real numbers
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
, the operator
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{\ell},\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}})
, and we have

\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{\ell},\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}})}\leqslant 2^{\frac{(k+1)|\ell-\ell^{\prime}|}{2}},

2.

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
,
\left[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}\right]=\left[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}\right]=\left[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}\right]=\left[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},(\Id-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}\right]=0,

3.

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
, we have
\left[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},P^{\alpha}\right]=0
, in particular we have
\left[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\right]=0
,

4.

for any
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\left[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\right]=\left[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},|D_{s}|^{\ell}\right]=\left[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},\partial_{s}\right]=0
,

5.

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, the operator
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
is a bounded self-adjoint operator on
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
,

6.

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, if
f\in\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(respectively
f\in\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
), the sequence
(\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}f)
converges to
f
in
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(respectively in
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
),

7.

\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}^{2}=\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
and
\mathsf{J}_{k}\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}=\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}\mathsf{J}_{k}=\mathsf{J}_{k}
.

The proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
4.1.3
and is left to the reader.

Remark 4.1.5
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in H^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cup\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
with
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
. According to Proposition
4.1.3
, Items 1 and 2, and the local Sobolev embedding
H^{2j}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow H^{j}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
with
j\in\mathbb{N}
(see for instance
[
21
]
, Theorem 4.16), we deduce that
\mathsf{J}_{k}f
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
. Similarly, if
f\in\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
with
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, then
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}f
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
.

4.2
The Leray projector on
\mathbb{H}^{d}

We now give some properties concerning the Leray projector on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, given in (
3.2
), that we recall for convenience

\mathbb{P}:=\Id+\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-1}\circ\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}.

Proposition 4.2.1
.

The Leray projector
\mathbb{P}
satisfies the following properties:

1.

\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}\circ\mathbb{P}=0
,

2.

if
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})^{2d}
is a horizontal vector field such that
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0
, then
\mathbb{P}u=u
,

3.

for any
p\in(1,+\infty)
, we have
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
,

4.

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
[\mathbb{P},(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}]=[\mathbb{P},(\Id-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}]=0
,

5.

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
, we have
[\mathbb{P},\tilde{P}^{\alpha}]=0
,

6.

for any positive real number
\zeta
and
\ell
, we have
[\mathbb{P},e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}]=[\mathbb{P},|D_{s}|^{\ell}]=0
,

7.

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
[\mathbb{P},\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}]=0
,

8.

\mathbb{P}
is self-adjoint on
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
,

9.

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, the operator
\mathbb{P}
is bounded on
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and on
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

Proof.

The first two points follow from direct computation. To prove Item 3, let us begin by remarking that
\Id-\mathbb{P}
is a
2d\times 2d
-matrix operators whose components are of the form
-P_{i}\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-1}\circ P_{j}
. Then the continuity follows from the continuity of the Riesz transforms
P_{i}\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and
(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\circ P_{j}
on
L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(see for instance
[
10
]
). Items
4
,
5
,
6
and
7
follow from Propositions
2.3.1
and
2.3.3
. Item 8 is a consequence of Items
3
(with
p=2
) and
4
. Finally, the last item follows from Proposition
2.4.2
and the continuity of
(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-1}
from
\dot{H}^{\ell+1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
\dot{H}^{\ell-1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.
∎

Remark 4.2.2
.

Let us note the
[\mathbb{P},\mathsf{J}_{k}]\neq 0
and
[\mathbb{P},P_{j}]\neq 0
for any
j\in[\![1,2d]\!]
. This contrasts with Items
5
and
7
of Proposition
4.2.1
.

We introduce the following crucial identity.

Lemma 4.2.3
.

Let
v
be a smooth enough horizontal vector field. If
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(v)=0
, then we have

(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})v=\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\circ\partial_{s}v,

(4.4)

where

\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}:=4(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ\mathfrak{S},

and
\mathfrak{S}
is the matrix defined in (
1.1
). Moreover,
\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}
is an operator of order
0
with respect to both left-invariant and right-invariant sub-Laplacians (see Definition
2.5.1
).

Proof of Lemma
4.2.3
.

Let us begin by remarking that for any smooth enough horizontal vector fields
v
, we have

\displaystyle\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant 2d}P_{i}P_{j}^{2}v_{i}=2\sum_{1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant 2d}[P_{i},P_{j}]P_{j}v_{i}+\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(v).

However, in view of (
2.3
) and since
\partial_{s}
commute with
X_{i}
and
\Xi_{i}
for any
i\in[\![1,d]\!]
(see (
2.4
)), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant 2d}[P_{i},P_{j}]P_{j}v_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}[X_{i},\Xi_{i}]\Xi_{i}v_{i}+\sum_{1\leqslant j\leqslant d}[\Xi_{j},X_{j}]X_{j}v_{j+d}

\displaystyle=-4\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}\partial_{s}(\Xi_{i}v_{i}-X_{i}v_{i+d})

\displaystyle=-2\left(\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}2(\Xi_{i}\partial_{s}v_{i}-X_{i}\partial_{s}v_{i+d})\right)

\displaystyle=-2\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathfrak{S}\partial_{s}v).

Thus, for
v
such that
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(v)=0
,

\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}v)=4\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathfrak{S}\partial_{s}v).

Then, (
4.4
) follows from the definition of
\mathbb{P}
. Because
(\Id-\mathbb{P})
is an operator of order
0
(see Proposition
4.2.1
, Items
8
and
9
), the operator
\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}
is also of order
0
.
∎

This Lemma will be crucial to prove the convergence of the solution of approximate systems and to propagate the regularity with respect to the left-invariant vector fields in Lemma
7.2.2
.

4.3
Stokes system

In this subsection, using the properties of the Leray projector on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
and the operators
\mathsf{J}_{k}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
, we investigate the well-posedness of the linear Stokes system in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Let
f
be in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
and
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
such that
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0
. Then, there exists a unique solution
u\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+},L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
of the following initial value problem

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u=\mathbb{P}f\ &\text{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\\
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0\ &\text{in}\ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\\
u_{|_{t=0}}=u_{0}\ &\text{in}\ \ \mathbb{H}^{d}.\end{cases}

(4.5)

Moreover, this solution satisfies, for any
T>0
,

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\langle f,u\rangle_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}.

(4.6)

Proof.

Let us take
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We consider the following regularized system

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{k}-\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}=\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(f\star_{t}\eta_{k}),\\
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{k})=0,\\
u_{k}{|_{t=0}}=u_{0},\end{cases}

(4.7)

where, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
\eta_{k}
is defined as follows: we choose
\eta\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{-})
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta=1
and we set
\eta_{k}:=\frac{1}{k+1}\eta(\frac{\cdot}{k+1})
and where we have extended
f
by
0
on
(-\infty,0)
. In view of the properties of
\mathbb{P}
(see Proposition
4.2.1
, Items 1 and 2) and
\mathsf{J}_{k}
(see Proposition
4.1.3
, Item 1), and the Young inequality in order to ensure that
f\star_{t}\eta_{k}
belongs to
L^{\infty}(\dot{H}^{-1})
, we deduce that (
4.7
) is the Cauchy problem for an ordinary differential equation on the Banach space

L^{2}_{\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}}:=\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})~\middle|~\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0\right\},

equipped with the
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
topology. Then, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
T_{k}>0
and a unique solution
u_{k}:[0,T_{k})\times\mathbb{H}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2d}
of (
4.7
) which belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,T_{k}),L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
. Moreover, we have

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u_{k}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\langle\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k},u_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(f\star_{t}\eta_{k}),u_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Since
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0
, we have
\mathbb{P}u_{k}=u_{k}
and by using that
\mathbb{P}
is a self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(see Proposition
4.2.1
, Item 8) and the properties of
\mathsf{J}_{k}
(see Proposition
4.1.3
, Items
4
,
5
and
7
), we get

-\langle\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k},u_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}}=-\langle\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k},\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

and

\langle\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(f\star_{t}\eta_{k}),u_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle f\star_{t}\eta_{k},\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\|f\star_{t}\eta_{k}\|_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Accordingly, we deduce that for any
t\in(0,T_{k})
, we have

\|u_{k}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}d\tau\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|(f\star_{t}\eta_{k})(\tau)\|_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}d\tau.

(4.8)

Then
u_{k}\in L^{\infty}((0,T_{k}),L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
and according to the properties of
\mathsf{J}_{k}
, we have for any
\tau\in(0,T_{k})

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u(\tau)+\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\mathsf{J}_{k}(f\star_{t}\eta_{k})(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}\|u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T_{k});L^{2})}+\|\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{-1},L^{2})}\|f\star_{t}\eta_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\dot{H}^{-1})}.

Hence, according to the blow-up criteria for ordinary differential equations, if
(0,T_{k})
is the maximal existence interval, this implies that
T_{k}=+\infty
. Since, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}

\int_{0}^{t}\|(f\star_{t}\eta_{k})(\tau)\|_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}d\tau\leqslant\|\eta_{k}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|f(\tau)\|_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}d\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\|f(\tau)\|_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}d\tau,

then the right-hand side of (
4.8
) is bounded independently on
k
. Thus, we deduce that, up to extract a subsequence,
(u_{k})
converges weakly-
\star
in
L^{\infty}(L^{2})
to a function
u
and
(\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
converges weakly in
L^{2}(\dot{H}^{1})
to a function
v
. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, since
u_{k}
and
\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}
belong to
L^{\infty}(L^{2})
, using that
\mathsf{J}_{k}
is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(see Proposition
4.1.3
, Item 5), for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
,

\langle\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k},\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\langle u_{k}(t),\varphi(t)\rangle_{L^{2}}dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\langle u_{k}(t),(\mathsf{J}_{k}-\Id)\varphi(t)\rangle_{L^{2}}dt.

Because
(u_{k})
converges weakly-
\star
to
u
in
L^{\infty}(L^{2})
and
((\mathsf{J}_{k}-\Id)\varphi)
converges strongly in
L^{1}(L^{2})
to
0
(using Proposition
4.1.3
, which ensure that
\|\mathsf{J}_{k}-\Id\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}\leqslant 2
, the convergence of
((\mathsf{J}_{k}-\Id)\varphi(t))
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and the dominated convergence theorem), we deduce that
(\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
converges to
u
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
. This ensures that
u=v
. By the same way, since
(f\star_{t}\eta_{k})
converges to
f
in
L^{2}(\dot{H}^{-1})
and using that
\mathbb{P}
is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we deduce that for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
,

\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\langle\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(f\star_{t}\eta_{k}),\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}}=\langle f,\mathbb{P}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(L^{2})}=\langle\mathbb{P}f,\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(L^{2})}.

This ensures that
u
is a solution of the Cauchy problem (
4.5
) in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Moreover, in view of the properties of the weak-
\star
and weak convergences, we deduce the estimate (
4.6
) holds for
u
by passing to the limit in (
4.8
). Finally, the continuity in time follows by interpolation since
\partial_{t}u
belongs to
L^{2}(\dot{H}^{-1})
according to the first line of (
4.5
). To prove the uniqueness, let us pick two solutions
u^{1}
and
u^{2}
of (
4.5
) that belong to
\mathcal{C}_{b}(L^{2})\cap L^{2}(\dot{H}^{1})
. Then, we have

\|u^{1}-u^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}(u^{1}-u^{2})\|_{L^{2}(L^{2})}^{2}\leqslant 0,

that is
u^{1}=u^{2}
.
∎

4.4
Construction of the approximate problem

We are next interested in the well-posedness of the following Cauchy problem in
\mathcal{C}_{b}(L^{2})\cap L^{2}(\dot{H}^{1})

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{k}-\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}+\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})=0\ &\text{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\\
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{k})=0\ &\text{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\\
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{k}=u_{k}\ &\text{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\\
\end{cases}

(4.9)

and

u_{k|_{t=0}}=\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\ \text{in}\ \ \mathbb{H}^{d},

(4.10)

where
u_{0}
is a horizontal vector field belonging to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H})
or
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H})
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. This is the object of the following lemma.

Lemma 4.4.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
u_{0}
be a horizontal vector field which belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cup\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and satisfies
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0
. Then there exists a unique solution
u_{k}
of (
4.9
)-(
4.10
), which belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+},L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
and satisfies

\|u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(L^{2})}^{2}\leqslant\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(4.11)

Moreover, we have
u_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\tilde{H}^{\ell})
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\in L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{\ell})
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular,
u_{k}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

The proof is an adaptation of the classical strategy: first we show local existence and second we show that all solutions are global by establishing a blow-up criteria. However, some difficulties appear due to the structure of the system and the fact that we consider two different regularities.

Proof.

In this proof, since
k
is fixed, we dropped the subscript
k
for
u
to simplify notations.

For any
T\in(0,+\infty)
, we define the space

E_{k}^{T}:=\left\{u\in\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))~\middle|~\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0\ \ \text{and}\ \ \tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u=u\right\},

which is a closed sub-space of the Banach spaces
\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
. Let us consider
T>0
which will be chosen later. Let us define the map
\Phi_{u_{0}}
, which from
v\in E_{k}^{T}
gives the unique solution
u
of the following Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u+\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}v)=0\ &\text{in}\ \ (0,T)\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\\
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0,\ &\text{in}\ \ (0,T)\times\mathbb{H}^{d},\end{cases}

(4.12)

and

u_{|_{t=0}}=\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\ \text{in}\ \ \mathbb{H}^{d}.

(4.13)

This map is well-defined according to Lemma
4.3.1
, since
\mathsf{J}_{k}(v\cdot\nabla\mathsf{J}_{k}v)
belongs to
L^{2}(\dot{H}^{-1})
when
v\in E_{k}^{T}
.

Local existence

1)
E_{k}^{T}
is stable by
\Phi_{u_{0}}
.
Let
v
be in
E_{k}^{T}
, and set
u:=\Phi_{u_{0}}(v)
. We have
u\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(L^{2})\cap L^{2}(\dot{H}^{1})
according to Lemma
4.3.1
and
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0
. Then, in order to show that
u
belongs to
E_{k}^{T}
, it is enough to show that

\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u=u.

(4.14)

Since
[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},\mathbb{P}]=[\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k},\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}]=0
and
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}\mathsf{J}_{k}=\mathsf{J}_{k}
(see Proposition
4.1.4
Items 2 and 7 and Proposition
4.2.1
Item 7), we deduce that
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u
satisfies the first line of (
4.12
). Using that
[\divergence_{\mathbb{H}},\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}]=0
(see Proposition
4.1.4
, Item 3), we obtain that
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u)=0
on
[0,T)
. Finally, we have
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{|_{t=0}}=\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}^{2}u_{0}=\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}
. Thus,
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u
is also a solution of (
4.12
)-(
4.13
). Since
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(L^{2})\cap\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{1})
, we have also
\tilde{u}\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(L^{2})\cap L^{2}(\dot{H}^{1})
. Then (
4.14
) follows from the uniqueness provided by Lemma
4.3.1
and then
u
belongs to
E_{k}^{T}
. We conclude that
\Phi_{u_{0}}
maps
E_{k}^{T}
to itself.

2) For
T>0
small enough,
\Phi_{u_{0}}
is a strict contraction on a subset of
E^{T}_{k}
.
Let
v
be in
E_{k}^{T}
. We have

\langle\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}v),v\rangle_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}v)\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\dot{H}^{-1})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2},

and then, in view of (
4.6
), it follows that

\|\Phi_{u_{0}}(v)\|_{E_{k}^{T}}^{2}:=\|\Phi_{u_{0}}(v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\Phi_{u_{0}}(v)\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}\\
\leqslant\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}v)\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\dot{H}^{-1})}^{2}.

(4.15)

Furthermore, according to Proposition
4.1.3
, Item 1 and Proposition
2.4.7
, we deduce that there exists a constant
C_{k}
, which does not depend on
T
such that

\|\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}v)\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\dot{H}^{-1})}^{2}\leqslant C_{k}T\|v\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}.

Then combining this inequality with (
4.15
), we deduce that

\|\Phi_{u_{0}}(v)\|_{E_{k}^{T}}^{2}\leqslant\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C_{k}T\|v\|_{E_{k}^{T}}^{2}.

If
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are two elements of
E_{k}^{T}
, then, by choosing
C_{k}
large enough, we deduce by the same way that

\|\Phi_{u_{0}}(v_{1})-\Phi_{u_{0}}(v_{2})\|_{E_{k}^{T}}^{2}\leqslant C_{k}T\|v_{1}-v_{2}\|_{E_{k}^{T}}(\|v_{1}\|_{E_{k}^{T}}+\|v_{2}\|_{E_{k}^{T}}).

It follows that, if we set

T(u_{0}):=\frac{1}{4C_{k}\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},

(4.16)

then the map
\Phi_{u_{0}}
is a strict contraction on
B_{u_{0}}:=\left\{u\in E_{k}^{T(u_{0})}~\middle|~\|u\|_{E_{k}^{T(u_{0})}}^{2}\leqslant 2\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right\}
.

3) Fixed-point argument
. According to the Banach fixed-point argument, the map
\Phi_{u_{0}}
admits a unique fixed-point on
B_{u_{0}}
which is a solution of (
4.9
)-(
4.10
) on
[0,T(u_{0})]
.

Global existence.

1) Energy estimate.
Let
T_{\star}>0
. We consider a solution
u
of (
4.9
)-(
4.10
) belonging to
E_{k}^{T_{\star}}
. Then, we have

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\langle\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u),u\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle(\Id-\mathbb{P})\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}}.

However, thanks to Proposition
4.2.1
, Items
2
and
8
, we deduce that the right-hand side of the above inequality vanishes. Additionally, since
\mathsf{J}_{k}
is self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(see Proposition
4.1.3
, Item
5
) we have

\langle\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u),u\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\mathsf{J}_{k}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u),u\rangle_{L^{2}}=-\frac{1}{2}\langle\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u),|\mathsf{J}_{k}u|^{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0.

Thus, it follows that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=0.

We deduce that

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{\star}}(L^{2})}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T_{\star}}(L^{2})}^{2}=\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(4.17)

2) Uniqueness.
Let
u^{1}
and
u^{2}
be two solutions of (
4.9
)-(
4.10
) belonging to
\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,T(u_{0})];L^{2})\cap L^{2}((0,T(u_{0}));\dot{H}^{1})
. According to (
4.17
),
u^{1}
and
u^{2}
belong to
B_{u_{0}}
, and then coincide on
[0,T(u_{0})]
by uniqueness of the fixed-point of
\Phi_{u_{0}}
. Let us denote by
T_{0}
the supremum of the time
t
so that
u^{1}=u^{2}
on
[0,t)
. We have
T_{0}\geqslant T(u_{0})>0
. Suppose that
T_{0}<T_{\star}
. By continuity, we have
u^{1}(T_{0})=u^{2}(T_{0})
. Thanks to the time translation invariance of (
4.9
), we deduce that
u^{1}(T_{0}+\cdot)
and
u^{2}(T_{0}+\cdot)
are two solutions of (
4.9
) with the same initial data (remark that
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u^{1}(T_{0})=u^{1}(T_{0})
) and belong to
E_{k}^{t}
for any
t\in(0,T_{\star}-T_{0})
. Then, performing the same energy inequality as (
4.17
) with
u^{1}(T_{0}+\cdot)
and
u^{2}(T_{0}+\cdot)
, we deduce that
u^{1}(T_{0}+\cdot)
and
u^{2}(T_{0}+\cdot)
are two fixed-points of
\Phi_{u^{1}(T_{0})}
that belong to
B_{u^{1}(T_{0})}
. In view of the uniqueness of the fixed-point of
\Phi_{u^{1}(T_{0})}
in
B_{u^{1}(T_{0})}
, we finally get
u^{1}=u^{2}
on
[0,T_{0}+T_{1}]
with
T_{1}:=\min\{T(u_{0}),T_{\star}-T_{0}\}>0
. This is in contradiction with the definition of
T_{0}
, and then
T_{0}=T_{\star}
.

3) Blow-up argument
. It follows from the uniqueness of the solution of (
4.9
)-(
4.10
) that there exists a maximal existence time denoted by
T_{\star}>0
. Suppose that
T_{\star}
is finite. If
t<T_{\star}
, then the solution
u(t+\cdot)
exists at least on
[0,T(u(t))]
(see (
4.16
)). Then, for any
t\in(0,T_{\star})
, we have
T(u(t))\leqslant T_{\star}-t
, that is

\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2\sqrt{C_{k}(T_{\star}-t)}}.

This implies that
\lim_{t\rightarrow T_{\star}}\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}}=+\infty
, which contradicts (
4.17
). We conclude that
T_{\star}=+\infty
.

The fact that
u_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\tilde{H}^{\ell})
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\in L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{\ell})
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, follows from Proposition
4.1.4
, Item 1, since
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{k}=u_{k}
and
u_{k}\in L^{\infty}(L^{2})\cap L^{2}(\dot{H}^{1})
. According to Remark
4.1.5
, we also deduce that
u(t)
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
for any
t>0
.
∎

Remark 4.4.2
.

In the proof, in order to show (
4.14
), we use that
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
commutes with
\mathbb{P}
and
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}
. These two properties are not satisfied by
\mathsf{J}_{k}
(see Remark
4.2.2
). This is the spirit of our work: the regularity with respect to
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
is propagated in the equation.

5
Existence of global weak solutions: Proof of Theorem
1.2.3

As the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, the
L^{2}
-energy estimate for (
4.9
)-(
4.10
) is the key argument to obtain Leray-type theorem, namely the existence of global weak solutions of finite energy with initial data in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Accordingly, we begin by establishing the following lemma that ensures that the sequence of solutions to the approximate systems remains bounded in the energy space if the initial data belongs to
L^{2}
.

Lemma 5.0.1
.

Let
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
be a horizontal vector field such that
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0
. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
u_{k}
the associate solution of (
4.9
)-(
4.10
). Then we have

\|u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(L^{2})}^{2}\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(5.1)

Proof of Lemma
5.0.1
.

Estimate
(
5.1
)
follows immediately from (
4.11
) since
\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}\leqslant 1
.
∎

We are now able to prove Theorem
1.2.3
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2.3
.

In this proof the constant implied by
\lesssim
is independent of
k
. From Lemma
5.0.1
, without loss of generality we can assume that
(u_{k})
converges weakly-
\star
in
L^{\infty}(L^{2})
and weakly in
L^{2}(\dot{H}^{1})
to
u
. Obviously, we have
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0
and the sequence
(\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k})
converges to
\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
. We also deduce that
(\partial_{t}u_{k})
converges to
\partial_{t}u
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

It remains to show that
(\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}))
converges to
\mathbb{P}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u)
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
. In view of the equation satisfied by
u_{k}
, that is (
4.9
)-(
4.10
), and Lemma
5.0.1
, we get

\|\partial_{t}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(H^{-Q/2})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(H^{-Q/2})}.

Since
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{k})=0
, we have
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k})=u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}
. Then, according to Proposition
4.1.3
, Item 1 and Proposition
4.2.1
, Item 9, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(H^{-\frac{Q}{2}})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\mathbb{P}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{-Q/2})}\|\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(H^{-Q/2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(H^{1-Q/2})}.

Using Proposition
4.1.3
, Item 1 and Proposition
2.4.7
in
\dot{H}^{1-Q/2}
, with
\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
u_{k}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, with

\|\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k}\|_{H^{1-Q/2}}\lesssim\|\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k}\|_{\dot{H}^{1-Q/2}}\lesssim\|\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}}.

Hence, thanks to Lemma
5.0.1
and using that
\|\mathsf{J}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}\leqslant 1
, we have

\|\partial_{t}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(H^{-Q/2})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+\|u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(L^{2})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Thus, according to the above bound for
(\partial_{t}u_{k})
, Lemma
5.0.1
and the embedding
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow H^{-Q/2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, it follows from the Aubin-Lions theorem (see
[
37
]
) and the Cantor diagonal argument that
(u_{k})
converges strongly in
L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
u
. Then, in view of the weak convergence of
(\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
to
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
in
L^{2}(L^{2})
, we deduce that
(\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}))
converges to
\mathbb{P}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u)
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
. This shows that
u
satisfies the momentum and the continuity equations in the sense of Definition
1.2.2
, Items
3
and
4
respectively. Let us pick
t
and
t^{\prime}
in
[0,+\infty)
. Let
v
be a horizontal vector field belonging to
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and satisfying
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(v)=0
. Thanks to the momentum equation in the sense of Definition
1.2.2
, Item
3
, we deduce that

|\langle u(t)-u(t^{\prime}),v\rangle_{L^{2}}|\leqslant|t-t^{\prime}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\dot{H}^{1})}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v\|_{L^{2}}+\|u\otimes u\|_{L^{2}(H^{1-Q/2})}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v\|_{L^{2}}\right).

(5.2)

Therefore, the left-hand side of (
5.2
) converges to
0
when
t
goes to
t^{\prime}
. Using that
\mathbb{P}
is a self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}
and that
\mathbb{P}u=u
, we deduce that the left-hand side of (
5.2
) converges to
0
, when
t
goes to
t^{\prime}
even when
v
simply is a (possibly non-divergence free) horizontal vector field in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Moreover, since
u\in L^{\infty}(L^{2})
, we get the same convergence result when
v
simply belongs to
L^{2}
by density.

This shows that
u
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{w}([0,+\infty),L^{2})
and satisfies the initial condition in the sense of Definition
1.2.2
, Item
2
. This concludes the proof.
∎

6
Well-posedness in
\tilde{H}^{d}
: proof of Theorem
1.2.4

In this section, we turn our attention to the case in which the initial data belong to
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

6.1
Existence of global solutions in
\tilde{H}^{d}

In this subsection, we will show the following theorem.

Theorem 6.1.1
.

There exists a positive real number
\varepsilon
such that for any horizontal vector field
u_{0}\in\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
satisfying
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0
and

\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}<\varepsilon,

(6.1)

there exists a solution
u\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d})
of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) satisfying

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant 2\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}.

6.1.1
\tilde{H}^{d}
energy estimates

This subsection is devoted to the proof of energy estimates for the solutions of System (
4.9
) in
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. The main idea is to propagate the
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
regularity in the energy in order to cancel the pressure, to use the Sobolev embedding for the two Sobolev-type scales
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\dot{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
alternatively, and to close energy estimates from classical bootstrap argument (which works because the linear term in the pressure does not appear during the process).

Lemma 6.1.2
.

If
\varepsilon>0
small enough and
u_{0}\in\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
satisfies (
6.1
), then, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the following energy estimate

\|u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant 2\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}

holds, where
u_{k}
denotes the solution of (
4.9
)-(
4.10
).

Proof.

Let
T>0
. In this proof we skip the index
k
on
u_{k}
and the constant implied by
\lesssim
is independent of
k
and
T
. Let us recall that
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u=u
. Furthermore, in view of Proposition
4.1.4
, Item 3, we have also
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u=\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
. Thus, in view of Proposition
4.1.4
Item 1,
u
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
respectively belong to
\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
and
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d}))
. Moreover, for any
t\in[0,+\infty)
, we have
u(t)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
(see Remark
4.1.5
). By taking the
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
scalar product of the first line of (
4.9
) and
u
, since
u
is smooth in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d}
, we deduce that, for any
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u(t)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}-\langle\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u(t),u(t)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}=-\langle\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u(t)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u(t)),u(t)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

By using that
\mathbb{P}
is a self-adjoint operator on
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and that
\mathbb{P}u=u
(see Proposition
4.2.1
, Items
2
and
8
), we get

-\langle\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u(t),u(t)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}=\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}.

Besides, by using that
\mathbb{P}u=u
and
\mathsf{J}_{k}
is self-adjoint on
\tilde{H}^{d}
(see Proposition
4.1.3
, Item
5
), we get

\displaystyle-\langle\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u(t)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u(t)),u(t)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}

\displaystyle=-\langle\mathsf{J}_{k}(u(t)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u(t)),u(t)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}

\displaystyle=-\langle(u(t)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u(t)),\mathsf{J}_{k}u(t)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

Therefore, for any
t^{\prime}
and
t
in
(0,T)
such that
t^{\prime}<t
, we have

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u(t^{\prime})\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(t^{\prime})\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}=-\langle u(t^{\prime})\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u(t^{\prime}),\mathsf{J}_{k}u(t^{\prime})\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

By integrating the above identity between
0
and
t
and taking the supremum on
[0,T]
, it follows that

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+2\int_{0}^{T}|\langle u_{k}(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}(\tau),\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}|d\tau.

(6.2)

We shall estimate the nonlinear term by using the following lemma that we will prove later.

Lemma 6.1.3
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for any function
a
and
b
in
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
c
such that
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c
belongs to
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we have

|\langle ab,c\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}|\leqslant C\|a\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|b\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

By using Lemma
6.1.3
and Proposition
4.1.3
, Items 1 and 3, we deduce from (
6.2
) that there is a constant
C_{\star}>0
, independent of the index
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
T>0
, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+C_{\star}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+C_{\star}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}.

Let us define

T_{\star}:=\sup\left\{T\geqslant 0~\middle|~\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant 2\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\right\}.

According to the continuity of
t\in[0,+\infty)\mapsto\|u(t)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}
(see Lemma
4.4.1
) and the properties of
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
, we have
T_{\star}>0
. Supposed that
T_{\star}<+\infty
. If
T\in(0,T_{\star})
, then it follows from the definition of
T_{\star}
and the smallness condition on
\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}
, that

\|u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+(2-\sqrt{2}C_{\star}\varepsilon)\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}.

Then, by choosing
\varepsilon\in(0,\sqrt{2}/C_{\star})
, we deduce by a standard bootstrap argument that
T_{\star}=+\infty
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
6.1.2
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.1.3
.

By density in
\tilde{H}^{d}
, without loss of generality, we can assume that
a
and
b
belong to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. First by using the Hölder estimate and the Sobolev embedding
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(recall Proposition
2.4.5
Item 3 and Remark
2.4.6
) , we get

\displaystyle|\langle ab,c\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}(ab)\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q+2}}}\|(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}c\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2}}}\lesssim\sum_{\gamma\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d}}\|\tilde{P}^{\gamma}(ab)\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q+2}}}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

Let
\gamma\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d}
. Thanks to the Leibniz formula and the Hölder estimates, we get

\|\tilde{P}^{\gamma}(ab)\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q+2}}}\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{d}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}\\
\beta\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d-\ell}\end{subarray}}\|\tilde{P}^{\alpha}a\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-\ell)}}}\|\tilde{P}^{\beta}b\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2\ell}}}.

Then, for all
\ell\in[\![0,d]\!]
, applying the Sobolev embedding
\tilde{H}^{d-\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-\ell)}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2\ell}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(recall Proposition
2.4.5
Item 3 and Remark
2.4.6
), it follows that for any
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
and
\beta\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d-\ell}
,

\|\tilde{P}^{\alpha}a\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-\ell)}}}\lesssim\|\tilde{P}^{\alpha}a\|_{\tilde{H}^{d-\ell}}\lesssim\|a\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \|\tilde{P}^{\beta}b\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2\ell}}}\lesssim\|\tilde{P}^{\beta}b\|_{\tilde{H}^{\ell}}\lesssim\|b\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
6.1.3
.
∎

6.1.2
Convergence

In this subsection we complete the proof of Theorem
6.1.1
by showing that the sequence
(u_{k})
converges in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
to a solution of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) satisfying the suitable energy estimates.

The first step is to get the convergence of the linear part. According to the Lemma
6.1.2
, using the weak compactness of spaces
L^{\infty}(\tilde{H}^{d})
and
L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d})
and identifying the limits in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we deduce that, up to extract a subsequence,
(u_{k})
converges weakly-
\star
to a function
u
and
(\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k})
converges weakly to
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
in
L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d})
. Moreover, thanks to the properties of the weak and the weak-
\star
convergence and the energy estimate from Lemma
6.1.2
, for any
T>0
, we have

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L_{T}^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant 2\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}.

Thanks to the weak convergence of
(u_{k})
to
u
and the continuity properties of
\mathbb{P}
(see Proposition
4.2.1
, Item 9), we have the following convergence in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\partial_{t}u_{k}=\partial_{t}u,\ \,\ \ \lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}=\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u\ \ \text{and}\ \ \divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u)=0.

Moreover, the sequence
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}
converges to
u_{0}
in
\tilde{H}^{d}
(see Proposition
4.1.4
, Item 6). To conclude, it is sufficient to show that
(\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}))
converges to
\mathbb{P}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u)
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
. To this aim, according to the classical strategy, we need to have a strong convergence on the sequence
(u_{k})
, that we can get by bounding
(\partial_{t}u_{k})
in a suitable space, using an Aubin-Lions type lemma and a locally compact embedding.

End of the proof of Theorem
6.1.1
.

We shall show the local (strong) compactness of the sequence
(u_{k})
in a suitable space. In this proof the constant implied by the symbol
\lesssim
is assumed to be independent of the parameter
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

1) If
\varphi
belongs to
\mathcal{D}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
, then
(\varphi u_{k})
is bounded in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})
.
By using the embedding
L^{\frac{Q}{2}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(see Proposition
2.4.5
, Item 3), we deduce that

\|\varphi u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|\varphi u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(L^{\frac{Q}{2}})}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(L^{Q})}\|u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{Q})}.

(6.3)

Hence, according to the Sobolev embedding
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{Q}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we obtain

\|\varphi u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\tilde{H}^{d})}.

According to Lemma
6.1.2
, it follows that

\|\varphi u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

(6.4)

Thus,
(\varphi u_{k})
is bounded in
L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})
.
Let us begin by remarking that, according to Lemma
4.2.3
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\varphi u_{k})

\displaystyle=(\partial_{t}\varphi)u_{k}+\varphi\mathbb{P}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}-\varphi\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})

\displaystyle=(\partial_{t}\varphi)u_{k}+\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}+\varphi\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{s}u_{k}-\varphi\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}).

Let us now estimate all the terms in
L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})
.

\bullet

Estimate on
(\partial_{t}\varphi)u_{k}
.
Since
\partial_{t}\varphi
belongs to
L^{2}(L^{Q})
, as for (
6.4
), we obtain

\|(\partial_{t}\varphi)u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

\bullet

Estimate on
\varphi\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
.
Using the embedding
L^{\frac{Q}{2}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, that
\varphi
belongs to
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
and the embedding
\tilde{H}^{d-1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{Q}{2}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we get

\|\varphi\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})}.

(6.5)

Since
\mathbb{P}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})
(see Proposition
4.2.1
, Item 8) and
\mathsf{J}_{k}
is bounded by
1
in this space (see Proposition
4.1.3
, Item 1), we have

\|\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})}\lesssim\|u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})}.

Then, by using the tame estimates of Proposition
2.4.7
for
u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}
on
\tilde{H}^{d-1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
with
u_{k}
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}
in
\tilde{H^{d}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(the fact that these two functions belong to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
follows from the continuity of the operator
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
from
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
from Proposition
4.1.4
, Item 1), since
\mathsf{J}_{k}
is bounded by
1
in
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{d})
, the energy estimates from Lemma
6.1.2
yield

\|\varphi\mathbb{P}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}.

\bullet

Estimate on
\varphi\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{s}u_{k}
.
Similarly to (
6.5
), we have

\|\varphi\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{s}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{s}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})}.

Let us recall that
\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}:=8(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ\mathfrak{S}
(see Lemma
4.2.3
). Thus, since
\mathbb{P}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})
, we deduce that
\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})
. Accordingly, we have

\|\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{s}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})}\lesssim\|\partial_{s}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})}.

Besides, according to Proposition
2.4.8
applied alternatively with
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\tilde{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we have

\|\partial_{s}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})}=\||D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})}\lesssim\||D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d})}\lesssim\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d})}.

Then, it follows from the energy estimates in Lemma
6.1.2
that

\|\varphi\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{s}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

\bullet

Estimate on
\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}
.
Since
\varphi
and
u_{k}
are smooth (this follows from Remark
4.1.5
and the third line of (
4.9
), namely
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{k}=u_{k}
), we can write

\displaystyle\|\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}

\displaystyle\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}+\|(\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\varphi)u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}.

(6.6)

By using the embedding
L^{\frac{Q}{2}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, the Hölder estimate and the embedding
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{Q}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we deduce from Lemma
6.1.2
that

\|(\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\varphi)u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

(6.7)

Besides, using the Leibniz rules, the Sobolev embedding and the energy estimate of Lemma
6.1.2
, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(L^{Q})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

Combining this estimate with (
6.6
) and (
6.7
), we deduce that

\|\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

We conclude that

\|\partial_{t}(\varphi u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}+\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}.

We conclude that
(\varphi u_{k})
is bounded in
H^{1}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})
.

2)
There exists a subsequence
(u_{k_{l}})
of
(u_{k})
such that for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
, the

sequence
(\varphi u_{k_{l}})
converges to
\varphi u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{Q})
.
According to the Cantor diagonal extraction, we are reduced to show that if
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
, there is a subsequence
(u_{k_{l}})
of
(u_{k})
such that
(\varphi u_{k_{l}})
converge to
\varphi u
in
L^{2}(L^{Q})
. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Since
(\varphi u_{k})
is smooth in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d}
and bounded in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})
, for any
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have

\|(\varphi u_{k})(\tau)-(\varphi u_{k})(\tau^{\prime})\|_{W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}}\lesssim|\tau-\tau^{\prime}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{t}(\varphi u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}\lesssim_{\varphi,u_{0}}|\tau-\tau^{\prime}|^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We deduce that
(\varphi u_{k})
is equicontinuous in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with value in
W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}
. From the energy estimate, the Hölder estimate and the embedding
\tilde{H}^{d}\hookrightarrow L^{Q}
, it follows that
(\varphi u_{k})
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(L^{Q})
. Applying Proposition
C.0.1
to
(\varphi u_{k}(t))
for
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we deduce that
(\varphi u_{k}(t))
is relatively compact in
W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Thanks to the Arzela-Ascoli theorem, we conclude that, up to extraction, that
(\varphi u_{k})
converge to
\varphi u
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})
. The next step is to show that
(\varphi u_{k})
converges in fact to
\varphi u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{Q})
. We will need the following lemma (the proof is an adaptation of the proof
[
12
, (A.17) p. 195]
and is left to the reader).

Lemma 6.1.4
.

Let
E
,
F\subset F^{\prime}
three Banach spaces,
A\in\mathcal{L}(E,F)
and
B\in\mathcal{L}(F,F^{\prime})
such that
R(A)\subset N(\Id-B)
. Assume that
A
is a compact operator. Then, for all
\eta>0
, there exists
C_{\eta}>0
such that for all
u\in E
, we have

\|Au\|_{F}\leqslant\eta\|u\|_{E}+C_{\eta}\|B\circ Au\|_{F^{\prime}}.

Choose a bounded open subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
such that
supp(\varphi)\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega
.
We now introduce
\chi_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\chi_{1}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
such that
\chi_{0}=1
on
\Omega
, and
\chi_{1}=1
on the support of
\chi_{0}
, so that
\chi_{1}\chi_{0}=\chi_{0}
.
From Proposition
C.0.1
, the multiplication by
\chi_{0}
is a compact operator from
W^{1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}
to
L^{Q}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Lemma
6.1.4
then provides, for any
\eta>0
, a constant
C_{\eta}>0
such that for any
v\in W^{1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}
,

\|\chi_{0}v\|_{L^{Q}}\leqslant\eta\|v\|_{W^{1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}}+C_{\eta}\|\chi_{0}v\|_{W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}}.

It follows that for all
t>0
,

\|\varphi(t,\cdot)v\|_{L^{Q}}\leqslant\eta\|v\|_{W^{1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}}+C_{\eta}\|\varphi(t,\cdot)v\|_{W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}}.

Let us consider
T>0
so that
supp(\varphi)\subset[0,T]\times\mathbb{H}^{d}
. Let
\eta>0
. Since
(\varphi u_{k})
converge in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})
, there is
N_{\eta,T}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
l,k\geqslant N_{\eta,T}
we have

\|\varphi u_{k}-\varphi u_{l}\|_{L^{\infty}(W^{-1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}<\frac{\eta}{C_{\eta}T^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Then, using the Sobolev embedding and the energy estimate of Lemma
6.1.2
, we have, for any
k
and
l
larger than
N_{\eta,T}
,

\|\varphi u_{k}-\varphi u_{l}\|_{L^{2}(L^{Q})}\leqslant\eta\|\varphi u_{k}-\varphi u_{l}\|_{L^{2}(W^{1,Q}_{\mathbb{H}})}+\eta\lesssim_{\varphi,u_{0}}\eta.

This shows that
(\varphi u_{k})
converges to
\varphi u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{Q})
.

3)
Convergence of the nonlinear term.
Let us begin by showing that, up to extract a subsequence,
(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
converges to
u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
. We pick
\chi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
so that
\chi=1
on
supp(\varphi)
. Since
u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}
belongs to
L^{2}(L^{\frac{Q}{2}})
, we can write

\langle u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k},\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d}}(\varphi u_{k})\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\,dtdx.

(6.8)

Using the Hölder estimate and the Sobolev embedding
\tilde{H}^{d}\hookrightarrow L^{Q}
, we deduce that
(\varphi\chi u_{k})
is strongly convergent to
\varphi u=\varphi\chi u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{\frac{Q}{Q-1}})=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{Q})^{{}^{\prime}}
(because
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{Q/2})
) and
(\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
is weakly convergent to
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{Q})
. We deduce from (
6.8
) and from the properties of the weak convergence (see
[
14
, Proposition 3.5, Item 4, p. 58]
) that

\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\langle u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k},\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d}}\varphi\left(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\right)dtdx.

We have proved that
(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
converges to
u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

Since the sequence
(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
is bounded in
L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})
, we deduce from Proposition
4.1.3
, Item
6
, that
(\mathsf{J}_{k}-\Id)(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
converges weakly to
0
in
L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})
and, up to extract a subsequence, that
(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
converges weakly to
u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
in
L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d-1})
. According to the continuity of
\mathbb{P}
on
\tilde{H}^{d-1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, this ensures that
u
satisfies (
1.5
).

4)
Continuity in time.
We finish the proof by showing the continuity in time for the solution, that is
u\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d})
. Since
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}u
belongs to
L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{1})
and
\partial_{t}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}u\in L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{-1})
, we deduce by interpolation that
u
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d})
.
∎

6.2
Stability and uniqueness

In this section our goal is to establish the stability of solutions of (
1.5
).

Theorem 6.2.1
.

There exists a positive constant
C
such that for any
T>0
and for any solutions
u
and
v
of (
1.5
) such that
u
and
v
belong to
\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,T];\tilde{H}^{d})
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v
belong to
L^{2}([0,T];\tilde{H}^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\|u-v\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}(u-

\displaystyle v)\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|u(0)-v(0)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\exp{\left(C\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+C\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}\right)}.

From this result we deduce immediately the uniqueness of the solutions constructed in Theorem
6.1.1
. This thus concludes the proof of Theorem
1.2.4
, Items 1 and 2.

Proof.

In this proof the constant implied by
\lesssim
is independent of
T
. Without loss of generality, up to regularize
u
and
v
with respect to the time and space variable, we can assume that
u
and
v
are smooth. Let us set
w:=v-u
and, for any
t\in[0,T]
,

e(t):=\|w(t)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}.

Let
t\in[0,T]
. By developing the expression of
e(t)
, we obtain

e(t)=\|u(t)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+\|v(t)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+2\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}-2E(t),

(6.9)

where

E(t):=\langle v(t),u(t)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}+2\int_{0}^{t}\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau),\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau.

Besides, by a direct calculus, using the equation satisfied by
u
and
v
, and keeping in mind that
[(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}},\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]=0
in order to cancel the pressure, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau),\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau=

\displaystyle\langle v(0),u(0)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}-\langle v(t),u(t)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}-\int_{0}^{t}\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau),\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\langle v(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau),u(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau-\int_{0}^{t}\langle v(\tau),u(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau,

that is

E(t)=\langle v(0),u(0)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}-\int_{0}^{t}\langle v(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau),u(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau-\int_{0}^{t}\langle v(\tau),u(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau.

In view of (
6.9
) and by computing the energy for
u
and
v
in
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, it follows that

\displaystyle e(t)

\displaystyle=\|u(0)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+\|v(0)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}-2\langle v(0),u(0)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}

\displaystyle\ \ -2\int_{0}^{t}\left(\langle u(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau),u(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}+\langle v(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau),v(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\right)d\tau

\displaystyle\ \ +2\int_{0}^{t}\left(\langle v(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau),u(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}+\langle u(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau),v(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\right)d\tau

\displaystyle=\|w(0)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+2\int_{0}^{t}\left(\langle w(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau),w(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}+\langle v(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w(\tau),w(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\right)d\tau.

(6.10)

We have to estimate

\int_{0}^{t}\langle w(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau),w(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau+\int_{0}^{t}\langle v(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w(\tau),w(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau.

(6.11)

By applying Lemma
6.1.3
, we have

\left|\int_{0}^{t}\langle w(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau),w(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau\right|\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\|w(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau.

In order to recover the estimate on the second term of (
6.11
), let us remark that, if
d
is even, then

(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{d}=\mathcal{Z}^{2},\ \text{ where }\mathcal{Z}:=(\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}=\sum_{i\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\frac{d}{2}}}\prod_{j=1}^{\frac{d}{2}}\tilde{P}_{i_{j}}^{2},

and if
d
is odd, then we have

(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{d}=-\sum_{h=1}^{2d}(\mathcal{M}\tilde{P}_{h})(\tilde{P}_{h}\mathcal{M}),\ \text{ where }\mathcal{M}:=(\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d-1}{2}}=\sum_{i\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\prod_{j=1}^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\tilde{P}_{i_{j}}^{2}.

Note that
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{M}
are (unbounded) self-adjoint operators in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. At first, let us assume that
d
is even. Then, we have

\langle v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w,w\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}=\langle\mathcal{Z}(v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w),\mathcal{Z}w\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathcal{Z}w,\mathcal{Z}w\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle[\mathcal{Z},v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]w,\mathcal{Z}w\rangle_{L^{2}}.

However, if
a
and
b
are two smooth horizontal vector fields, such that
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(a)=0
, then

\langle a\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}b,b\rangle_{L^{2}}=0.

Thus, we have

\langle v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w,w\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}=\langle[\mathcal{Z},v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]w,\mathcal{Z}w\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Let us now assume that
d
is odd. Similarly to the case where
d
is even, we get

\langle v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w,w\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}=\sum_{h=1}^{2d}\langle[\tilde{P}_{h}\mathcal{M},v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]w,\tilde{P}_{h}\mathcal{M}w\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Furthermore, let us note that
\mathcal{Z}
and
\tilde{P}_{h}\mathcal{M}
with
h\in[\![1,2d]\!]
commute with
P_{j}
, for any
j\in[\![1,2d]\!]
, and are both a sum of terms of the form
\tilde{P}^{\gamma}
where
\gamma
belongs to
[\![1,2d]\!]^{d}
. Then, in order to get an estimate on
\langle v\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w,w\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}
, we shall use the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2.2
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for any smooth enough horizontal vector fields
a
,
b
and
c
with
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(a)=0
and for all
\gamma\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d}
, we have

|\langle[\tilde{P}^{\gamma},a\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]b,c\rangle_{L^{2}}|\leqslant C\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}a\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|b\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c\|_{L^{2}}.

We will give the proof of this lemma after the proof of Theorem
6.2.1
.

By using Lemma
6.2.2
, we deduce that

\left|\int_{0}^{t}\langle v(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w(\tau),w(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau\right|\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|w(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau.

and

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\langle w(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau),w(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}

\displaystyle+\langle v(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w(\tau),w(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}d\tau\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\|w(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\left(\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\right)d\tau.

Thus, from (
6.10
) and the Young estimate, it follows that

e(t)\leqslant\|w(0)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+C_{\star}\int_{0}^{t}\|w(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\left(\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\right)d\tau+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}w(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}d\tau,

for some positive constant
C_{\star}
, which is independent of
t
,
u
and
v
. We conclude that the following estimate

\displaystyle\|w(t)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle w(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}d\tau

\displaystyle\leqslant\|w(0)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+C_{\star}\int_{0}^{t}\|w(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\left(\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}v(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\right)d\tau,

holds for any
t\in[0,T]
. Finally, Theorem
6.2.1
follows from the Gronwall estimate.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.2.2
.

Let
\gamma\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d}
and
a
,
b
and
c
, three smooth enough horizontal vector fields on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
with
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(a)=0
. Then, according to (
2.8
),
[\tilde{P}^{\gamma},a\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}]b
is the sum of terms of the form

\tilde{P}^{\alpha}a\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\tilde{P}^{\beta}b,

with
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
and
\beta\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d-\ell}
where
\ell
belongs to
[\![1,d]\!]
, according to
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(a)=0
, we have

\tilde{P}^{\alpha}a\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\tilde{P}^{\beta}b=\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(\tilde{P}^{\beta}b\otimes\tilde{P}^{\alpha}a).

Hence, we get

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{P}^{\alpha}a\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\tilde{P}^{\beta}b,c\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i,j\in[\![1,2d]\!]}\langle\tilde{P}^{\alpha}a_{i},\tilde{P}^{\beta}b_{j}P_{i}c_{j}\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\tilde{P}^{\alpha}a\|_{\dot{W}^{1,\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-\ell)}}_{\mathbb{H}}}\|\tilde{P}^{\beta}b\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c\|_{\dot{W}^{-1,\frac{2Q}{Q+2(d-\ell)}}_{\mathbb{H}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\tilde{P}^{\alpha}a\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-\ell)}}}\|\tilde{P}^{\beta}b\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c\|_{\dot{W}^{-1,\frac{2Q}{Q+2(d-\ell)}}_{\mathbb{H}}}.

Furthermore, since
\ell\neq 0
, we have the Sobolev embedding
L^{\frac{Q}{Q-\ell}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow\dot{W}_{\mathbb{H}}^{-1,\frac{2Q}{Q+2(d-\ell)}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(see Proposition
2.4.5
, Item 3), and then thanks to the Hölder estimate, we deduce that

\|\tilde{P}^{\beta}b\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c\|_{\dot{W}^{-1,\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-\ell)}}}\lesssim\|\tilde{P}^{\beta}b\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c\|_{L^{\frac{Q}{Q-\ell}}}\lesssim\|\tilde{P}^{\beta}b\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2\ell}}}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c\|_{L^{2}}.

We conclude the proof of Lemma
6.2.2
by using the Sobolev embedding
\tilde{H}^{d-\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-\ell)}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2\ell}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.
∎

7
Smoothing effects: proof of Theorem
1.2.5

7.1
Vertical regularity

Let us begin by establishing some basic facts about the characterization of analyticity with respect to the vertical variable
s
by using the Fourier transform on the Heisenberg group.

7.1.1
Vertical analyticity and Fourier transform on
\mathbb{H}^{d}

Let
\zeta
be a positive real number. Let
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
so that the following map

\mathsf{T}_{\zeta}:(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}\mapsto e^{\zeta|\lambda|}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda),

belongs to
L^{2}(\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d})
. When this definition makes sense, we write

e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}f:=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}^{-1}(\mathsf{T}_{\zeta}f).

As suggested by the Fourier inversion formula on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, we aim to make the link between the Fourier multiplier
e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}
on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
and the Euclidean Fourier multiplier
e^{\zeta|\Lambda|}
of symbol
e^{\zeta|\xi_{s}|}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
. It is well known that the operator
e^{\zeta|\Lambda|}
acts on a space of analytic functions with respect to the vertical variable
s
.

Remark 7.1.1
.

Let
p\in(1,+\infty)
and
\zeta\geqslant 0
. We define the two following spaces

\mathcal{A}^{p,\zeta}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\left\{f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})~\middle|~e^{\zeta|\Lambda|}f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\right\}\ \text{and}\ \ \mathcal{A}^{p,\zeta}_{\mathbb{H}}:=\left\{f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})~\middle|~e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\right\}.

These two spaces are equal and we denote them by
\mathcal{A}^{p,\zeta}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. In fact, we have, for any
f\in\mathcal{A}^{p,\zeta}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
,

e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}f=e^{\zeta|\Lambda|}f.

(7.1)

Proof of Remark
7.1.1
.

With
W
defined by the formula (
2.21
),
if
f
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, then
for any
(n,m,\lambda)\in\tilde{\mathbb{H}}^{d}
, we have

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}W(n,m,\lambda,Y)\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}}(f(Y,\cdot))(\lambda)dY.

Thus, if
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, then
e^{-\zeta|\Lambda|}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(e^{-\zeta|\Lambda|}f)(n,m,\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}W(n,m,\lambda,Y)e^{-\zeta|\lambda|}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}}(f(Y,\cdot))(\lambda)dY=e^{-\zeta|\lambda|}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(n,m,\lambda).

We deduce that
e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}e^{-\zeta|\Lambda|}=\Id
on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. We conclude by using the density of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.
∎

7.1.2
Analytic smoothing effects in the vertical variable

Continuing our study of the Cauchy theory for solutions of (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) for initial data in the critical space
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we now show that the solutions of (
1.5
) are instantaneously smoothed with respect to the variable
s
. More precisely, we aim to show the following theorem.

Theorem 7.1.2
.

Let
\sigma\in(0,4d)
. There exists
\varepsilon_{\sigma}>0
such that for any initial data
u_{0}\in\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
satisfying
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0
and
\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}<\varepsilon_{\sigma}
, there exists a unique solution
u
of (
1.5
) satisfying
u\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d})
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\tilde{H}^{d})
and the following energy estimate

\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u\|_{L^{\infty}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{4d}\right)\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}.

(7.2)

Moreover, there is a positive constant
A
, which is independent of
\sigma
, such that
\varepsilon_{\sigma}=A(1-\sigma/4d)
.

In other words, for any
t>0
, the radius of analyticity (see (
1.9
) for the definition) of
u(t)
with respect to the variable
s
is bounded from below by
\sigma t
.

Proof.

The uniqueness of such solution follows from Theorem
6.2.1
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sigma\in(0,4d)
and
u_{0}\in\tilde{H}^{d}
. Let us consider the solution
u_{k}
of the approximate problem (
4.9
)-(
4.10
). In this proof the constant implied by
\lesssim
is independent of
k
and the time variable. By using the Plancherel theorem on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, we deduce that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}-\sigma\||D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}=\langle e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}\partial_{t}u_{k},e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

Besides, by computing the right-hand side of the above estimate thanks to (
4.9
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}-\sigma\|

\displaystyle|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}-\langle e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}),e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

Due to the following estimate

\||D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{4d}\|(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}=\frac{1}{4d}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2},

we get

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{4d}\right)\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\leqslant|\langle e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}),e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}|.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}

\displaystyle\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+2\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{4d}\right)\|e^{\sigma\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}|\langle e^{\sigma\tau|D_{s}|}(u_{k}(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}(\tau)),e^{\sigma\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}|d\tau.

(7.3)

We now derive an estimate on the nonlinear term by using the following lemma that we shall temporarily assume (The proof will be given after the proof of Theorem
7.1.2
).

Lemma 7.1.3
.

There exists a constant
C_{d}
such that for any positive real numbers
\zeta
and for every function
a
,
b
and
c
such that
e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}a
,
e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}b
and
e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c
belong to
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we have

|\langle e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}(ab),e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}c\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}|\leqslant C_{d}\|e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}a\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}b\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}c\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

Note that the constant
C_{d}
is independent of
\zeta
, which is essential in order to get a suitable energy estimate (
7.2
) for global solutions.

We are now able to complete the proof of Theorem
7.1.2
. By applying Lemma
7.1.3
, Proposition
4.1.3
, Items 1 and 3, and using that
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}
is bounded by
1
in
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{d})
(see Proposition
4.1.4
, Item 1), we deduce from (
7.3
) that for any
t\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+2\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{4d}\right)

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+B\|e^{\sigma\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(\tilde{H}^{d})}\|e^{\sigma\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}_{t}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2},

(7.4)

where
B
is a positive constant which is independent of
\sigma
.

We can now perform the standard bootstrap argument. Let us define

T_{\star}:=\sup\left\{T\geqslant 0~\middle|~\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant 2\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\right\}.

We shall first show that
T_{\star}>0
. Since
u_{k}=\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{k}
, we have, for any
(n,m,\lambda)
in
\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}
and
t\geqslant 0
,

e^{\sigma|\lambda|t}|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{k}(t))(n,m,\lambda)|\leqslant\mathbf{1}_{\{|\lambda|\leqslant 2^{k}\}}e^{\sigma|\lambda|t}|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{k}(t))(n,m,\lambda)|\leqslant e^{\sigma 2^{k}t}|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{k}(t))(n,m,\lambda)|.

Then, according to the Plancherel formula on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
(see Proposition
2.2.1
), we deduce that

\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}(t)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\leqslant e^{\sigma t2^{k}}\|u_{k}(t)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

Furthermore, by using the continuity of
t\mapsto u_{k}(t)
from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
into
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we deduce that there exists
t_{k}>0
, such that
e^{\sigma 2^{k+1}t_{k}}\leqslant\frac{3}{2}
and
\|u_{k}(t_{k})\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}\leqslant\frac{4}{3}\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}
. Then, since
\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\tilde{H}^{d})}\leqslant 1
, we deduce that
T_{\star}\geqslant t_{k}>0
. Let us now assume that
T_{\star}<+\infty
and show a contradiction for a suitable choice of
\varepsilon_{\sigma}
. Let
0<T<T_{\star}
. By definition of
T_{\star}
, we have

\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}\leqslant\sqrt{2}\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

Thus it follows from (
7.4
) and the smallness condition on
\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}
, that

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+2\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{4d}\right)\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{\sigma}B\sqrt{2}\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L_{T}^{2}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}.

(7.5)

Then, if we chose
\varepsilon_{\sigma}:=\frac{1-\frac{\sigma}{4d}}{\sqrt{2}B}
, thanks to (
7.5
), we obtain

\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{4d}\right)\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}.

In view of the definition of
T_{\star}
, by the classical continuity argument this shows that
T_{\star}=+\infty
, and then the above estimate holds with
T=+\infty
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then we finish the proof by passing to the limit
k\to+\infty
as in the proof of Theorem
6.1.1
.
∎

We now prove Lemma
7.1.3
. The proof relies on the multilinear Calderon-Zygmund theory (see
[
30
, Lemma 24.8, p. 252]
) to recover an estimate on the nonlinear term
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}(ab)
. More precisely, in this paper we use an anisotropic version of
[
30
, Lemma 24.8, p. 252]
, that we will now establish.
We aim to study continuity properties of the following bilinear operators, defined for
\zeta\geqslant 0
,
A
and
B
in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
(Y,s)\in\mathbb{H}^{d}
by

\displaystyle M_{\zeta}(A,B)(Y,s)
