\displaystyle:=e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\left((e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}A)(e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}B)\right)(Y,s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{is(\lambda+\mu)}e^{\zeta(|\lambda+\mu|-|\lambda|-|\mu|)}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}}(A(Y,\cdot))(\lambda)\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}}(B(Y,\cdot))(\mu)d\lambda d\mu.

Lemma 7.1.4
.

Let
p
,
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
in
(1,+\infty)
satisfying
1/p=1/p_{1}+1/p_{2}
. Then, there exists a constant
K
such that for any
\zeta\geqslant 0
and for all
A
in
L^{p_{1}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
B
in
L^{p_{2}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we have
e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\left((e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}A)(e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}B)\right)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and

\|e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\left((e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}A)(e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}B)\right)\|_{L^{p}}\leqslant K\|A\|_{L^{p_{1}}}\|B\|_{L^{p_{2}}}.

(7.6)

Let us give the main arguments of the proof of Lemma
7.1.4
(which is an adaptation of the proof of
[
30
, Lemma 24.8, p. 252]
) for the sake of clarity, since we use both Fourier transforms on
\mathbb{R}
and on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
in our analysis.

Sketch of the proof of Lemma
7.1.4
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

K_{1}f(s):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{is\lambda}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}}(f)(\lambda)d\lambda\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ K_{-1}f(s):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{is\lambda}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}}(f)(\lambda)d\lambda.

Moreover, for any
\zeta>0
and any
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, we introduce

L_{\zeta,1}f:=f\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ L_{\zeta,-1}f:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{is\lambda}e^{-2\zeta|\lambda|}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}}(f)(\lambda)d\lambda.

Finally, we set for any
\zeta>0
and
\alpha,\beta\in\{-1,1\}

\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}:=\Id_{\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\otimes K_{\alpha}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathsf{Z}_{\zeta,\alpha,\beta}:=\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}\big(\Id_{\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\otimes L_{\zeta,\alpha\beta}\big),

where we denote by
\Id_{\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}
the identity operator on
\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
. Then, we have

M_{\zeta}(A,B)=\sum_{(\gamma,\beta,\alpha)\in\{-1,1\}^{3}}\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{\zeta,\alpha,\beta}A\cdot\mathsf{Z}_{\zeta,\alpha,\gamma}B\right).

Operators
\mathsf{K}_{\alpha}
and
\mathsf{Z}_{\zeta,\alpha,\beta}
are Fourier multipliers of order zero, hence they are bounded in any
L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
with
p\in(1,+\infty)
. The main point is the following: the family
\mathsf{Z}_{\zeta,\alpha,\beta}
is uniformly bounded with respect to the parameter
\zeta>0
in any
L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
with
p\in(1,+\infty)
. Indeed, the kernel of
L_{\zeta,-1}
is
\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{2\zeta}{s^{2}+4\zeta^{2}}:=k_{\zeta}(s)
and we have
\|k_{\zeta}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=\|k_{1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}
, so that by Young’s estimate with respect to the
s
variable, we deduce that for any
p\in(1,+\infty)
and for any
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
,

\|(\Id_{\mathcal{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\otimes L_{\zeta,-1})f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})}\leqslant\|k_{1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})}.

Then Lemma
7.1.4
follows from the Hölder estimate.
∎

We are now able to prove Lemma
7.1.3
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1.3
.

Let us begin by setting
A:=e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}a
and
B:=e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}b
so that
A
and
B
belong to
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Assume that
A
and
B
belong to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Then,
(e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}A)(e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}B)
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and for any
\gamma\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d}
, by using the Leibniz formula, we get

\left|e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\tilde{P}^{\gamma}\left((e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}A)(e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}B)\right)\right|\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{d}\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}\\
\beta\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d-\ell}\end{subarray}}\left|e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\left(e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}(\tilde{P}^{\alpha}A)e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}(\tilde{P}^{\beta}B)\right)\right|.

(7.7)

If we pick
\ell\in[\![0,d]\!]
,
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
,
\beta\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d-\ell}
and
\gamma\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d}
, applying Lemma
7.1.4
, we deduce that

\left|\!\left|e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\left(e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}(\tilde{P}^{\alpha}A)e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}(\tilde{P}^{\beta}B)\right)\right|\!\right|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q+2}}}\leqslant C_{1}\|\tilde{P}^{\alpha}A\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-\ell)}}}\|\tilde{P}^{\beta}B\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2\ell}}},

where
C_{1}
is a positive constant which is independent of
\zeta
. Applying the Sobolev embeddings
\tilde{H}^{d-\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-\ell)}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2\ell}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, and using
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
and
\beta\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d-\ell}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{P}^{\alpha}A\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-\ell)}}}\lesssim\|A\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \|\tilde{P}^{\beta}B\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2\ell}}}\lesssim\|B\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}.

In view of (
7.7
), it follows that

\left|\!\left|(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}\left(e^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}Ae^{-\zeta|D_{s}|}B\right)\right|\!\right|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q+2}}}\leqslant C_{2}\|A\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|B\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}},

(7.8)

where
C_{2}
depend only on the constant
C_{1}
,
d
and the constants of the Sobolev embeddings. By density of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
in
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, thanks to (
7.8
), we conclude that (
7.8
) holds for any
A
and
B
in
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. In addition, by applying the Hölder estimate, we deduce that

\left|\langle e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}(ab),e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}c\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\right|\leqslant C_{2}\|e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}a\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}b\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}e^{\zeta|D_{s}|}c\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2}}}.

We conclude by using the Sobolev embedding
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.
∎

We have proved that the analytic smoothing effect holds at least in the vertical variable. In the next section, we will improve the natural smoothing effect from the left-invariant sub-Laplacian provided by the control of the horizontal gradient of the solution in Theorem
6.1.1
.

7.2
Horizontal regularity

We now analyze the smoothness of the solutions
u
of (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) given by Theorem
7.1.2
with respect to the horizontal vector fields
(P_{j})_{j\in[\![1,2d]\!]}
, thus with respect to all variables.

Corollary 7.2.1
.

Let
u
be a solution of (
1.5
) constructed in Theorem
7.1.2
. Then for any nonnegative integers
\ell
,
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
and
t>0
, we have

P^{\alpha}\partial_{s}^{\beta}u(t)\in\tilde{H}^{d}.

(7.9)

Furthermore,
u
and
p
belong to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
. Accordingly, such solution
u
of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) is a strong solution of (
1.5
).

For any
\sigma\in(0,4d)
, we set

\sigma_{r}:=\frac{\sigma}{2^{r}},\ \ \ r\in\mathbb{N}.

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
and
\ell^{\prime}\in[0,Q/2)
, we introduce the space
H_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
by setting

H_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbb{H}^{d}):=\left\{f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})~\middle|~(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell}{2}}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell^{\prime}}{2}}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\right\},

and we denote by
\|\cdot\|_{H_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}}
the corresponding norms and by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}}
the corresponding scalar product.

Lemma 7.2.2
.

Let
u
be a solution of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) constructed in Theorem
7.1.2
with corresponding radius of analyticity bounded from below by
t\mapsto\sigma t
for
\sigma\in(0,4d)
, and
(u_{k})
the sequence of solutions of the approximate problems (
4.9
)-(
4.10
) which converges to
u
. Let
T
and
T_{\star}
be two real numbers such that
T>T_{\star}>0
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that there exist
T_{r}\in(0,T_{\star})
and a positive constant
C_{r}
such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\|e^{\sigma_{r}t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}([T_{r},T];H_{r,d})}+\int_{T_{r}}^{T}\|e^{\sigma_{r}t|D_{s}|}u_{k}(t)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}dt\leqslant C_{r}.

(7.10)

Then there exist
T_{r+1}\in[T_{r},T_{\star})
and a positive constant
C_{r+1}
such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}([T_{r+1},T];H_{r+1,d})}^{2}+\int_{T_{r+1}}^{T}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D|}u_{k}(t)\|_{H_{r+2,d}}^{2}dt\leqslant C_{r+1}.

(7.11)

Proof of Lemma
7.2.2
.

Energy estimate
:
Thanks to (
7.10
), we deduce that

\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\ \exists t_{k}\in\left(T_{r},\frac{T_{r}+T_{\star}}{2}\right)\ \ \ \|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t_{k}|D_{s}|}u_{k}(t_{k})\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}\leqslant\frac{4C_{r}}{(T_{\star}-T_{r})}.

(7.12)

Indeed, if this is not the case, there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
t\in\left(T_{r},(T_{\star}+T_{r})/2\right)
, we have

\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}u_{k}(t)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}>\frac{4C_{r}}{(T_{\star}-T_{r})}.

This would entail, since
(T_{\star}+T_{r})/2<T_{\star}<T
, that

\int_{T_{r}}^{T}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t_{k}|D_{s}|}u_{k}(t)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}dt>\frac{4C_{r}}{(T_{\star}-T_{r})}\times\frac{T_{\star}-T_{r}}{2}=2C_{r},

which would contradict (
7.10
).

Now, let us consider two integers
a
and
k
. Recall that
\mathsf{J}_{a}^{2}=\mathsf{J}_{a}
and that
\mathsf{J}_{a}
is self-adjoint (see Proposition
4.1.3
Items 5 and 7). Thanks to the commutation properties of
\mathsf{J}_{a}
(see Proposition
4.1.3
Items 3 and 4) and using that
\mathsf{J}_{a}
is bounded by
1
in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2})
, for any
t\in\left((T_{r}+T_{\star})/2,T_{\star}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(t)

\displaystyle\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}+2\left(1-\frac{\sigma_{r+1}}{4d}\right)\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau

\displaystyle\leqslant\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t_{k}|D_{s}|}u_{k}(t_{k})\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int_{t_{k}}^{t}|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})u_{k}(\tau),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(\tau)\rangle_{H_{r+1,d}}|d\tau

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int_{t_{k}}^{t}|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}(\tau)),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(\tau)\rangle_{H_{r+1,d}}|d\tau.

Let us take
t\in[t_{k},T]
. According to (
7.12
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(t)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}+2

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\sigma_{r+1}}{4d}\right)\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4C_{r}}{(T_{\star}-T_{r})}+I_{1}^{a,k}(t)+I_{2}^{a,k}(t),

(7.13)

where

I_{1}^{a,k}(t):=\int_{t_{k}}^{t}|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})u_{k}(\tau),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(\tau)\rangle_{H_{r+1,d}}|d\tau

and

I_{2}^{a,k}(t):=\int_{t_{k}}^{t}|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}(\tau)),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(\tau)\rangle_{H_{r+1,d}}|d\tau.

Estimate on
I_{1}^{a,k}(t)
.
Let us recall that the Leray projector
\mathbb{P}
(defined in (
3.2
)) is of order
0
. Accordingly,
(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})
is of order
2
which implies that to estimate
I_{1}^{a,k}(t)
, we need to control
e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}
at least in
L^{2}((t_{k},T);H_{r+2,d})
. Unfortunately, the condition (
7.10
) gives only a control of
e^{\sigma_{r}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}
in
L^{2}((t_{k},T);H_{r+1,d})
. We thus need to gain one additional regularity level with respect to the horizontal derivative, to the price of possibly losing some regularity in the vertical variable, since
\sigma_{r}>\sigma_{r+1}
. The key idea to overcome this difficulty is to take advantage of the equation
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{k})=0
, so that we can use Lemma
4.2.3
and the identity
(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})u_{k}=\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\circ\partial_{s}u_{k}
, where
\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}
is of order
0
and commutes with
|D_{s}|
.
This yields

\displaystyle I_{1}^{a,k}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{k}}^{t}|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\partial_{s}\circ\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}(\tau),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(\tau)\rangle_{H_{r+1,d}}|d\tau

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}d\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{t_{k}}^{T}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau.

(7.14)

In order to bound
e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{k}
in
L^{2}((t_{k},T);H_{r+1,d})
we use Hypothesis (
7.10
): Indeed, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
and
\tau
larger than
t_{k}
, we have

e^{2\sigma_{r+1}\tau|\lambda|}|\lambda|\leqslant\frac{e^{2\sigma_{r+1}\tau|\lambda|}(2\sigma_{r+1}\tau|\lambda|)}{2\sigma_{r+1}t_{k}}\leqslant\frac{e^{4\sigma_{r+1}\tau|\lambda|}}{2\sigma_{r+1}t_{k}}=\frac{e^{2\sigma_{r}\tau|\lambda|}}{\sigma_{r}t_{k}}\leqslant\frac{e^{2\sigma_{r}\tau|\lambda|}}{\sigma_{r}T_{r}}.

Thus, using the Plancherel formula on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, we get

\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}_{H_{r+1,d}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\sigma_{r+1}T_{r}}\|e^{\sigma_{r}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}.

Then, it follows from (
7.14
) and (
7.10
) that there exists a constant
C_{I_{1}}
independent of
k
such that for any
t\in[t_{k},T]
,

I_{1}^{a,k}(t)\lesssim\int_{T_{r}}^{T}\|e^{\sigma_{r}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau\leqslant C_{I_{1}}.

(7.15)

Regularity of
u_{k}
.
Let us first remark that, according to the properties of
\mathsf{J}_{a}
, we have

\displaystyle I_{2}^{a,k}(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{T}|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}\circ\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}(\tau)\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}(\tau)),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}(\tau)\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}|d\tau

\displaystyle\leqslant T^{\frac{1}{2}}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}\circ\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\tilde{H}^{d})}.

Since
\mathbb{P}
commutes with
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}
and
e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}
, and belongs to
\mathcal{L}(H^{r+1})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}\circ\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}(

\displaystyle u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\tilde{H}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\mathbb{P}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{r+1})}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}_{T}(H^{r+1})}.

Furthermore, it follows from Proposition
4.1.3
, Item
1
, that
\mathsf{J}_{k}e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k})
belongs to
L^{2}_{T}(H^{r+1})
. Then, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C_{k}
such that

\sup_{a\in\mathbb{N},t\in[t_{k},T]}\{I^{a,k}_{2}(t)\}\leqslant C_{k}.

Thus, according to (
7.10
) and (
7.15
), it follows from (
7.13
) that the two quantities

\sup_{a\in\mathbb{N}}\{\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(t_{k},T;H_{r+1,d})}^{2}\}

and

\sup_{a\in\mathbb{N}}\{\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(t_{k},T;H_{r+1,d})}^{2}\}

are finite. Let us set

F_{k}(t):=e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}u_{k}(t),

with
t\in[t_{k},T]
. By using that
\mathsf{J}_{a}
commute with
e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}
and
(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and a weak compactness argument, we deduce that, up to extract a subsequence and by identifying the limits in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((t_{k},T)\times\mathbb{H}^{d})
, the sequences
(\mathsf{J}_{a}F_{k})_{a\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\mathsf{J}_{a}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}F_{k})_{a\in\mathbb{N}}
converge, respectively, in
L^{\infty}(t_{k},T;L^{2})
for the weak-
\star
topology to
F_{k}
, and in
L^{2}(t_{k},T;L^{2})
for the weak topology to
(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}F_{k}
. Moreover, it follows from
(
7.12
)
and
(
7.15
)
that for any
t\in(t_{k},T)
, we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{a\rightarrow+\infty}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}

\displaystyle\|_{L^{\infty}(t_{k},t;H_{r+1,d})}^{2}

\displaystyle+2\left(1-\frac{\sigma_{r+1}}{4d}\right)\liminf_{a\rightarrow+\infty}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(t_{k},t;H_{r+1,d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4C_{r}}{(T_{\star}-T_{r})}+C_{I_{1}}+\sup_{a\in\mathbb{N}}\{I^{a,k}_{2}(t)\}.

Thus according to the properties of the weak and the weak-
\star
convergence, we deduce that for any
t\in(t_{k},T)
, we have

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}u_{k}(t)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}+2

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\sigma_{r+1}}{4d}\right)\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4C_{r}}{(T_{\star}-T_{r})}+C_{I_{1}}+\sup_{a\in\mathbb{N}}\{I^{a,k}_{2}(t)\}.

(7.16)

We are thus reduced to estimating
I^{a,k}_{2}(t)
uniformly with respect to the parameters
a\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Estimate on
I_{2}^{a,k}(t)
.
Let us begin by remarking that
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{k})=0
implies that

u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}=\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k}).

(7.17)

Besides, let us write

\displaystyle(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\circ\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}\circ\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle=(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\frac{r+1}{2}}\circ\Gamma_{k}\circ(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1},

(7.18)

with

\Gamma_{k}:=(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}\circ\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}\circ\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}\circ(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-(r+1)}.

Note that
\Gamma_{k}
is an operator of order
1
with respect to the left-invariant sub-Laplacian, which maps
2d\times 2d
matrix value functions to horizontal vector fields, and thus its adjoint

\Gamma_{k}^{*}=-(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-(r+1)}\circ\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}\circ\mathbb{P}\circ(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1},

is also of order 1 with respect to the left-invariant sub-Laplacian and maps horizontal vector fields to
2d\times 2d
matrix value functions. Then, using (
7.17
), (
7.18
) and the binomial expansion on
(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}
, we get

\displaystyle|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}

\displaystyle u_{k}),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\rangle_{H_{r+1,d}}|

\displaystyle=|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}\circ\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k}),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\rangle_{H_{r+1,d}}|

\displaystyle=|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}(\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k}),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\Gamma_{k}^{*}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{r+1}\sum_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}}|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}P^{\gamma}(\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k}),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}P^{\gamma^{\prime}}\Gamma_{k}^{*}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}|.

Furthermore, if we take
\ell\in[\![0,r+1]\!]
and
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}P^{\gamma}(

\displaystyle\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k}),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}P^{\gamma^{\prime}}\Gamma_{k}^{*}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\rangle_{\tilde{H}^{d}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}P^{\gamma}(u\otimes u)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\Gamma^{*}_{k}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\|_{H_{\ell,d}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d}}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\gamma}}P^{\gamma}(\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k})\|_{L^{2}}\right)\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\Gamma_{k}^{*}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}.

On one hand, using that
\mathbb{P}
commutes with
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\Gamma_{k}^{*}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}

\displaystyle=\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}\circ\mathbb{P}\circ(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}

\displaystyle=\|(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}\circ\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{a}\circ(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}}.

Then, using that
\mathsf{J}_{k}
and
\mathsf{J}_{a}
are bounded by
1
in
\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{1})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\Gamma_{k}^{*}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\mathbb{P}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{1})}\|(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Id-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{r+1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbb{P}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\dot{H}^{1})}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}.

On the other hand, by applying the Leibniz formula for
P^{\gamma}
with
\gamma\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell}
and for
\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\gamma}}
with
\tilde{\gamma}\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d}
, we deduce that

\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}P^{\gamma}\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\gamma}}(\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes u_{k})\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell\\
0\leqslant j\leqslant d\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{i}\\
\beta\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell-i}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\alpha}\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{j}\\
\tilde{\beta}\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d-j}\end{subarray}}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\alpha}}P^{\alpha}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\beta}}P^{\beta}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}}.

Then we have

\displaystyle|\langle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\circ\mathsf{J}_{k}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}),e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{a}u_{k}\rangle_{H_{r+1,d}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \times\sum_{\ell=0}^{r+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant i\leqslant\ell\\
0\leqslant j\leqslant d\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{i}\\
\beta\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell-i}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\alpha}\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{j}\\
\tilde{\beta}\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d-j}\end{subarray}}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\alpha}}P^{\alpha}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\beta}}P^{\beta}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}}.

(7.19)

Let us point out that the Calderón-Zygmund theory using in Lemma
7.1.4
cannot be applied in order to obtain a suitable estimate on
I_{2}^{a,k}(t)
in the cases
(i,j)=(r+1,d)
and
(i,j)=(0,0)
. Indeed, in these cases, we would like to choose one of the indexes
p_{1}
or
p_{2}
in Lemma
7.1.4
as
1
or
+\infty
, which is not allowed. In order to work around this difficulty, we will use interpolation inequalities.
Let us fix
\theta\in(0,1)
. Let
\ell\in[\![0,r+1]\!]
,
i\in[\![0,\ell]\!]
,
j\in[\![0,d]\!]
,
\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{i}
,
\tilde{\alpha}\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{j}
,
\beta\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{\ell-i}
and
\tilde{\beta}\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{d-j}
. Then, using Lemma
7.1.4
, we get

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\alpha}}P^{\alpha}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\beta}}

\displaystyle P^{\beta}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\alpha}}P^{\alpha}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-j+\theta)}}}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\beta}}P^{\beta}u_{k}\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(j+1-\theta)}}}.

Thanks to the Sobolev embeddings
\tilde{W}^{d-j,\frac{2Q}{Q-2\theta}}_{\mathbb{H}}\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-j+\theta)}}
and
\tilde{W}^{j,\frac{2Q}{Q-2(1-\theta)}}_{\mathbb{H}}\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(j+1-\theta)}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\alpha}}P^{\alpha}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(d-j+\theta)}}}\lesssim\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{i}{2}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2\theta}}}

and

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\beta}}P^{\beta}u_{k}\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(j+1-\theta)}}}\lesssim\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell-i}{2}}u_{k}\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(1-\theta)}}}.

Furthermore, using interpolation, the Sobolev embedding
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and the properties of
\mathsf{J}_{k}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}(-

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{i}{2}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2\theta}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{i}{2}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}}^{1-\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{i}{2}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\|^{\theta}_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{H_{i,d}}^{1-\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{H_{i,d}}^{\theta}

and

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle)^{\frac{d}{2}}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell-i}{2}}u_{k}\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2(1-\theta)}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell-i}{2}}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{d}{2}}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\frac{\ell-i}{2}}u_{k}\|_{L^{\frac{2Q}{Q-2}}}^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{H_{\ell-i,d}}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{H_{\ell-i,d}}^{1-\theta}.

Then, it follows that

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}(\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\alpha}}P^{\alpha}\mathsf{J}_{k}u_{k}\otimes\tilde{P}^{\tilde{\beta}}P^{\beta}u_{k})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{H_{i,d}}^{1-\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{H_{i,d}}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{H_{\ell-i,d}}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|^{1-\theta}_{H_{\ell-i,d}}.

(7.20)

We have to distinguish three cases in order to estimate the right-hand side of (
7.20
).

\bullet

Case
\ell<r+1
or
\ell=r+1
and
0<i<r+1
.
In this case, we have
i\leqslant r
and
\ell-i\leqslant r
. It then follows that

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{H_{i,d}}^{1-\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{H_{i,d}}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{H_{\ell-i,d}}^{\theta}\|

\displaystyle e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{H_{\ell-i,d}}^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|^{2}_{H_{r+1,d}}.

\bullet

Case
\ell=r+1
and
i=0
.
In this case, since
\sigma_{r+1}<\sigma_{r}
, according to (
7.10
), we get

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{1-\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{r}^{1-\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{1-\theta}.

\bullet

Case
i=\ell=r+1
.
Similarly as above, it follows from (
7.10
) that

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{1-\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}

\displaystyle\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{r}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2(1-\theta)}.

Thus, according to (
7.19
) and (
7.20
), we deduce that there exists a positive constant
C_{\star}
independent of
k
and
a
such that

\displaystyle I_{2}^{a,k}(t)\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{\star}\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau

\displaystyle+C_{\star}\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2-\theta}d\tau

\displaystyle+C_{\star}\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{1+\theta}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2(1-\theta)}d\tau.

In view of
\theta\in(0,1)
, by using the Young estimates with
1/2+1/2=1
,
\theta/2+(2-\theta)/2=1
and
(1+\theta)/2+(1-\theta)/2=1
we deduce that there exists a positive constant
\tilde{C}_{*}
, such that

\displaystyle I_{2}^{a,k}(t)\leqslant

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\sigma_{r+1}}{4d}\right)\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}(\tau)\|^{2}_{H_{r+1,d}}d\tau

\displaystyle+\tilde{C}_{*}\int^{t}_{t_{k}}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{4}d\tau.

(7.21)

Conclusion.
By combining (
7.14
) and (
7.21
), according to (
7.13
), we conclude that for all
t\in(t_{k},T)
,

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}u_{k}(t)

\displaystyle\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{\sigma_{r+1}}{4d}\right)\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4C_{r}}{(T_{\star}-T_{r})}+C_{I_{1}}+\tilde{C}_{*}\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{4}d\tau.

We deduce that there exists a positive constant
C^{\star}
independent of
k
such that for all
t\in(t_{k},T)
,

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}u_{k}(t)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}+\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{\star}+C^{\star}\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}(\tau)\|^{4}_{H_{r+1,d}}d\tau.

(7.22)

By applying the Gronwall lemma, we deduce from (
7.22
), (
7.10
),
\sigma_{r+1}<\sigma_{r}
and
t_{k}>T_{r}
that

\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}([t_{k},T];H_{r+1,d})}^{2}\leqslant C^{\star}e^{C^{\star}\int_{t_{k}}^{T}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau}\leqslant C^{\star}e^{C^{\star}C_{r}}.

Thus, according to (
7.22
), we conclude that for any
t\in(t_{k},T)

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}u_{k}(t)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}+\int_{t_{k}}^{t}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau\leqslant C^{\star}+C^{\star}e^{C^{\star}C_{r}(T-t_{k})}.

Then, by setting

T_{r+1}:=\sup\{t_{k}\}\in\left(T_{r},T_{\star}\right),

since
T_{r}<t_{k}\leqslant T_{r+1}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}t|D_{s}|}u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}([T_{r+1},T];H_{r+1,d})}^{2}+\int_{T_{r+1}}^{T}\|e^{\sigma_{r+1}\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u_{k}(\tau)\|_{H_{r+1,d}}^{2}d\tau\leqslant C^{\star}+C^{\star}e^{C^{\star}C_{r}(T-T_{r})}.

This complete the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 7.2.3
.

The proof of Lemma
7.2.2
contains two main difficulties: the main difficulty due to the term
(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})
appears in the estimate of the term
I^{a,k}_{1}
; in order to handle
I_{2}^{a,k}
, we have to use the operators
\mathsf{J}_{a}
to show that each
u_{k}
belongs to the appropriate space of regularity with respect to the left invariant sub-Laplacian
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
, so that it can be used to absorb a part of the nonlinear term with the diffusive term, see (
7.21
).

Now, we are able to show Corollary
7.2.1
.

Proof of Corollary
7.2.1
.

We shall perform a bootstrap argument in order to show that the solution is smooth. Let us consider two real numbers
T_{\star}
and
T^{\star}
such that
T^{\star}>T_{\star}>0
. According to (
7.2
), we deduce that there exists a positive real number
C_{0}
such that

\|e^{\sigma t|D_{s}|}u\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\star}];\tilde{H}^{d})}+\int_{0}^{T^{\star}}\|e^{\sigma\tau|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u(\tau)\|_{\tilde{H}^{d}}^{2}d\tau\leqslant C_{0}.

By reasoning by induction using Lemma
7.2.2
and passing to the limit in
(u_{k})
, up to extract a subsequence, we deduce that for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}([T_{\star},T^{\star}];H_{r,d})}\lesssim 1.

(7.23)

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2d+1}
. There exists a family of polynomial function, on the horizontal variable
Y
,
(\mu_{\beta,\gamma})_{(\beta,\gamma)\in\Gamma_{\alpha}}
where
\Gamma_{\alpha}
is a subset of
\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{|\alpha|}[\![1,2d]\!]^{i}\right)\times[\![1,|\alpha|]\!]
, such that

\partial^{\alpha}=\sum_{(\beta,\gamma)\in\Gamma_{\alpha}}\mu_{\beta,\gamma}P^{\beta}\partial_{s}^{\gamma}.

Thus, for any open bounded subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
and for any
t\in[T_{\star},T^{\star}]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\alpha}u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{|\alpha|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{i}\\
\gamma\in[\![1,|\alpha|]\!]\end{subarray}}\|P^{\beta}\partial_{s}^{\gamma}u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}.

(7.24)

We have a continuous embedding from
W^{2,Q}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
into
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
. By using the Sobolev embedding of
\tilde{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
into
L^{Q}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we conclude that
H_{2,d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
is continuously embedding in
\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
. Thus, we deduce from (
7.24
) and Proposition
2.4.8
, that for any
t\in[T_{\star},T^{\star}]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\alpha}u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{|\alpha|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in[\![1,2d]\!]^{i}\\
\gamma\in[\![1,|\alpha|]\!]\end{subarray}}\|P^{\beta}\partial_{s}^{\gamma}u(t)\|_{H_{2,d}}\lesssim\|u(t)\|_{H_{3|\alpha|+2,d}}.

It follows from (
7.23
) that for any
t\in[T_{\star},T^{\star}]
, we have
\partial^{\alpha}u(t)\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Since
T_{\star}<T^{\star}
,
\alpha
and
\Omega
are arbitrarily chosen, this shows that for any
t>0
,
u(t)
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
. Since
p
is explicitly given by (
3.1
), by hypoellipticity (see
[
27
]
) of the left-invariant sub-Laplacian
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
, we also have that for all
t>0
,
p(t)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
.
Besides, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have

\partial_{t}^{\ell}u=\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{t}^{\ell-1}u-\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}\binom{\ell-1}{k}\partial_{t}^{k}u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{t}^{\ell-1-k}u-\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{t}^{\ell-1}p.

By induction on
\ell
, we deduce that for any
t>0
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\partial_{t}^{\ell}u(t)
, and then, according to the hypoellipticity of
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
,
\partial_{t}^{\ell}p(t)
, belong to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
. We conclude that
u
and
p
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
.
∎

8
Long time existence in
\dot{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})

In this subsection, we discuss the existence of solution for (
1.5
) in
\dot{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, and explain how to obtain long time existence results in this framework.

Theorem 8.0.1
.

Let
T_{\star}>0
. Then there exist
a>0
and
\varepsilon>0
such that for any horizontal vector field
u_{0}\in\dot{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
with
\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0
and
\|e^{a|D_{s}|}u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}<\varepsilon
, there exists a solution
u
of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) satisfying
u\in\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,T_{\star}];\dot{H}^{d})
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\in L^{2}((0,T_{\star});\dot{H}^{d})
.

Note that Theorem
8.0.1
requires the initial datum to have some analyticity properties with respect to the vertical variable. Also note that the uniqueness of the solution of (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) satisfying
u\in\mathcal{C}_{b}([0,T_{\star}];\dot{H}^{d})
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\in L^{2}((0,T_{\star});\dot{H}^{d})
is an open problem.

Proof.

We only give the
a priori
estimates on smooth solution. The the convergence is left to the reader. Let
a>0
, which will be chosen later and
u_{0}
be a horizontal vector field in
\dot{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
so that

\divergence_{\mathbb{H}}(u_{0})=0\ \ \text{and}\ \ \|e^{a|D_{s}|}u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}<\varepsilon,

for some
\varepsilon>0
small enough which we will fix later in the proof. Let us consider a decreasing positive function
\delta
in
\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
(which depend on the time variable
t
). Let
u
a solution of (
4.9
)-(
4.10
) for some index
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We have

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}-\dot{\delta}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}+\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}\\
=-\langle e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{k}(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})u,e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\rangle_{\dot{H}^{d}}-\langle e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{k}\mathbb{P}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u),e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\rangle_{\dot{H}^{d}}.

(8.1)

Since
(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})u=\Pi_{\mathbb{H}}\partial_{s}u
and thanks to Proposition
4.1.3
Items 3, 4 and 5, we deduce that there exists a constant
C_{\star}
which does not depend on
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

|\langle e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{k}(\Id-\mathbb{P})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})u,e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\rangle_{\dot{H}^{d}}|\leqslant C_{\star}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}|D_{s}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}.

Then, it follows from Proposition
2.4.8
and (
8.1
) that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{(\dot{\delta}+C_{\star})}{4d}\right)\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}\leqslant-\langle e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{k}\mathbb{P}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u),e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\rangle_{\dot{H}^{d}}.

(8.2)

Using Proposition
4.1.3
Items 3, 4 and 5 and performing the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
6.1.3
we deduce that

\displaystyle|\langle e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\mathsf{J}_{k}\mathbb{P}(u\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\mathsf{J}_{k}u),e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\rangle_{\dot{H}^{d}}|\leqslant C^{\star}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}.

(8.3)

Accordingly, using (
8.2
) and (
8.3
), we get

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{(\dot{\delta}+C_{\star})}{4d}\right)\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}\leqslant C^{\star}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}.

(8.4)

We now choose
T_{\star}>0
,
a
and
\delta
a positive function in
\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that

C_{\star}+\max_{t\in[0,T_{\star}]}\{\dot{\delta}(t)\}<4d\ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \delta(0)=a.

This can be done for instance by choosing
\delta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
C_{\star}+\delta_{1}<4d
,
a\geqslant\max\{0,-\delta_{1}T_{\star}\}
, and
\delta(t)=a+\delta_{1}t
.

Then, if we set
\delta_{\star}:=(C_{\star}+\max_{t\in[0,T_{\star}]}\{\dot{\delta}(t)\})/4d
, we deduce that for any
T
in
[0,T_{\star}]
, we have

\displaystyle\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\dot{H}^{d})}^{2}+2(1-\delta_{\star})

\displaystyle\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\dot{H}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|e^{a|D_{s}|}u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}+2C^{\star}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\dot{H}^{d})}\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}(\dot{H}^{d})}^{2}.

We deduce from bootstrap type arguments that for
\varepsilon>0
chosen small enough, the following inequality holds

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{\star}}(\dot{H}^{d})}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T_{\star}}(\dot{H}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{\star}}(\dot{H}^{d})}^{2}+\|e^{\delta|D_{s}|}\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}u\|_{L^{2}_{T_{\star}}(\dot{H}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant\|e^{a|D_{s}|}u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{d}}^{2}.

Using a compactness argument, we deduce from the above energy inequality that there is a solution
u
in
\mathcal{C}([0,T_{\star}];\dot{H}^{d})
so that
u_{|_{t=0}}=u_{0}
and the above inequality holds.
∎

The proof of Theorem
8.0.1
suggests the following important remark. In view of (
8.4
), if the constants
C_{\star}
is strictly smaller than
4d
, the analyticity assumption on the initial datum is not needed: in such case, one could then take
a=0
and
\delta_{1}\in(0,4d-C_{\star})
, and
\delta(t)=\delta_{1}t
in the above proof. In this sense, the size of
C_{\star}
measures the loss of regularity (or dissipation) generated by the term
(\Id-\mathbb{P})\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}u
in
\dot{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. However, even if
C_{\star}<4d
, it is not clear that we can obtain an analog of the stability estimate of Theorem
6.2.1
in the
\dot{H}^{d}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
framework. Indeed, we cannot obtain a commutator estimate similar to Lemma
6.2.2
with
P^{\alpha}
instead
\tilde{P}^{\alpha}
, essentially due to the fact that the commutators
[X_{j},\Xi_{j}]=-4\partial_{s}
, with
j\in[\![1,d]\!]
, are homogeneous left-invariant operators of order two (see Lemma
2.4.8
).

9
Sub-Riemannian Euler and Navier-Stokes systems for general left-invariant structures

Let
G
be a stratified Lie group of step
r
and dimension
N
(which we identify to
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with a suitable group law) and let us denote by
\mathfrak{g}
its Lie algebra of left-invariant vector fields. We fix a stratification
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r}\mathfrak{g}_{j}
and we consider the Jacobian generators (see
[
13
, Definition 1.4.1, p. 56]
)
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N^{\prime}}
, with
N^{\prime}:=\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{1})\leqslant N
, of
\mathfrak{g}
, that is:
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N^{\prime}}
are generators of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathfrak{g}_{1}=\mathrm{Span}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N^{\prime}})
. Then, for any
j\in[\![1,N^{\prime}]\!]
, we have
Z_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}b_{k}^{j}\partial_{k}
, with
b_{j}^{j}=1
,
b_{k}^{j}=0
for any
k\in[\![1,N^{\prime}]\!]\setminus\{j\}
and, if
N^{\prime}<N
, for any
k\in[\![N^{\prime}+1,N]\!]
, the function
b_{k}^{j}
is polynomial and independent of
x_{k}
. Let us set
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}:=(b_{k}^{j})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant N,1\leqslant j\leqslant N^{\prime}}
. Then
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}},\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Now, if we consider the left-invariant sub-Riemannian structure on
G
, then
v=\!^{t}(v_{1},\dots,v_{N})
is a horizontal vector field if and only if
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}\!^{t}(v_{1},\dots,v_{N^{\prime}})=v
. We also say that
\!{}^{t}(v_{1},\dots,v_{N^{\prime}})
is a horizontal vector field. Generalizing the strategy of Section
1.1
to
G
(in such context
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}
plays the same role as
\mathcal{R}
) for the sub-Riemannian Euler and Navier-Stokes system on the Heisenberg group, we obtain the following system:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla_{G}u-\nu\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G}u=-\nabla_{G}p,\\
\divergence_{G}(u)=0,\end{cases}

(9.1)

where
\nu
belongs to
[0,+\infty)
,
\nabla_{G}:=\!^{t}\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}\nabla
,
\divergence_{G}:=\divergence(\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}\cdot)
and
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G}:=\divergence(\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}\!^{t}\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}\nabla\cdot)
. Let us remark that if
G=\mathbb{H}^{d}
, then
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{h}_{1}}=\mathcal{R}
. Furthermore, if
N^{\prime}=N
, then
G=(\mathbb{R}^{N},+)
,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}_{1}}
is the identity matrix and thus (
9.1
) is the incompressible Euler system on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
when
\nu=0
, or the incompressible Navier-Stokes system on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
when
\nu>0
. Moreover, for any smooth enough solution
(u,p)
of (
9.1
), we have

\frac{d}{dt}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\nu\|\nabla_{G}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=0.

Existence of weak solutions.

In this paragraph, we show that we can generalize Theorem
1.2.3
to the case of System (
9.1
) with
\nu>0
. Let us first give the definition of a global weak solution for the Cauchy problem (
9.1
) (which reduces to Definition
1.2.2
when
G
is the Heisenberg group and to the usual Leray solutions if
G=\mathbb{R}^{N}
).

Definition 9.0.1
.

Let
\nu>0
, and
u_{0}\in L^{2}(G)^{N^{\prime}}
be a horizontal vector field satisfying
\divergence_{G}(u_{0})=0
. We say that
u\in L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times G)^{N^{\prime}}
is a global weak solution of

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\nu\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G}u+u\cdot\nabla_{G}u=-\nabla_{G}p\ &\text{in}\ \ (0,+\infty)\times G,\\
\divergence_{G}(u)=0\ &\text{in}\ \ (0,+\infty)\times G\end{cases}

(9.2)

and

u_{|_{t=0}}=u_{0}\ \text{in}\ \ G,

(9.3)

if

1.

(Integrability conditions)
u
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{w}([0,+\infty);L^{2})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{2})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\dot{H}^{1}(G))
,

2.

(Initial condition)
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}u(t)=u_{0}
for the weak topology of
L^{2}(G)
,

3.

(Momentum equation) for any
t^{\prime}\leqslant t
in
[0,+\infty)
and for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}((0,+\infty)\times G)^{N^{\prime}}
such that
\divergence_{G}(\varphi)=0
, we have

\int_{G}u(t)\cdot\varphi(t)dx-\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\int_{G}\left(u\cdot\partial_{t}\varphi+\nu u\cdot\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G}\varphi+(u\otimes u)\cdot\nabla_{G}\varphi\right)dxd\tau=\int_{G}u(t^{\prime})\cdot\varphi(t^{\prime})dx,

4.

(Continuity equation) For all
t>0
, we have
\divergence_{G}(u(t))=0
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(G)^{N^{\prime}}
.

By adapting the proof of Theorem
1.2.3
(the proof is left to the reader), we obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 9.0.2
.

Let
\nu>0
and
u_{0}
be a horizontal vector field belonging to
L^{2}(G)
and satisfying
\divergence_{G}(u_{0})=0
. Then there exists a global weak solution
u
of (
9.2
)-(
9.3
), satisfying the following energy estimate

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}^{2}+2\nu\|\nabla_{G}u\|_{L^{2}(L^{2})}^{2}\leqslant\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

By setting
\mathbb{P}^{G}:=\Id+\nabla_{G}\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G})^{-1}\circ\divergence_{G}
, we can rewrite System (
9.2
) as follows

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\nu\mathbb{P}^{G}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G}u+\mathbb{P}^{G}(u\cdot\nabla_{G}u)=0,\\
\divergence_{G}(u)=0.\end{cases}

(9.4)

Let us now give the main elements allowing to prove Theorem
9.0.2
.

Analysis on stratified Lie group.

Let us denote by
\widehat{G}
the unitary dual of
G
. For any
\pi\in\widehat{G}
we denote by
H_{\pi}
the associated representation space and by
\mathscr{F}_{G}
the Fourier transform on
G
. Note that
H_{\pi}
is a Hilbert space and, thanks to the Kirillov theory (see
[
28
, Theorem 3, p. 103]
), can be realized as the space of square integrable functions on a Euclidean space. Now, for any
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, we define the right-invariant vector field
\tilde{X}
by
\tilde{X}f:=-X\check{f}
, where for any
w\in G
,
\check{f}(w):=f(w^{-1})
and
w^{-1}
denotes the inverse of
w
for the law of
G
. We also denote by
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{G}
the right-invariant sub-Laplacian on
G
define by
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{G}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{Z}_{j}^{2}
. Let
T\in\{-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G},Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N^{\prime}}\}
and
\tilde{T}\in\{-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{G},\tilde{Z}_{1},\dots,\tilde{Z}_{N^{\prime}}\}
. Then
T
and
\tilde{T}
commute. Moreover, if
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, then we have
\mathscr{F}_{G}(Xf)(\pi)=\mathscr{F}_{G}(f)(\pi)\circ\pi(X)
and
\mathscr{F}_{G}(\tilde{X}f)(\pi)=\pi(X)\circ\mathscr{F}_{G}(f)(\pi)
. Also, for any
\pi\in\widehat{G}
, the operator
\pi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G})
is a self-adjoint compact operator on a separable Hilbert space
H_{\pi}
and its spectrum
\{E(m,\pi)\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
lies in
(0,+\infty)
. Thus if we denote by
(h_{m}^{\pi})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
an orthonormal basis of
H_{\pi}
of eigenvectors of
\pi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G})
, then for any
f\in L^{1}(G)
, we can set

\mathcal{F}_{G}(f)(n,m,\pi):=\langle\mathscr{F}_{G}(f)(\pi)h_{m}^{\pi},h_{n}^{\pi}\rangle_{H_{\pi}},

for any
(n,m,\pi)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\times\widehat{G}
. (Note that, by using the Kirillov theory, we can parametrize
\widehat{G}
by the orbit of the coadjoint action from
G
on
\mathfrak{g}^{{}^{\prime}}
in order to obtain a more concrete description of the unitary dual, see
[
28
]
). Similarly as in Sections
2.3
and
2.4
, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the operators
(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G})^{\ell/2}:\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G})^{\ell/2})\subset L^{2}(G)\rightarrow L^{2}(G)
and we denote by
\dot{H}^{\ell}(G)
the closure of
\mathcal{S}(G)\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G})^{\ell/2}))
for the norm
\|(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G})^{\ell}\cdot\|_{L^{2}}
. By the same way, we define
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{G})^{\ell/2}
and
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(G)
, with
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular, Proposition
B.0.1
, Lemma
B.0.3
and the identities involving the sub-Laplacian in Proposition
2.3.1
can be adapted to
G
(the details are left to the reader). Thanks to classical results, Propositions
2.4.5
and
2.4.7
also hold on
G
(see
[
19
, Section 4]
).

Approximate problem.

Our strategy in this article, based on
a priori
estimates on suitable approximate problems, can be generalized to
G
as follows. We first introduce for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
the operators
\mathsf{J}_{k}^{G}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}^{G}
by setting for any
f\in\mathcal{S}(G)
and
(n,m,\pi)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\times\widehat{G}

\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathsf{J}_{k}^{G}f)(n,m,\pi):=\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant E(n,\pi)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}(n,m,\pi)\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant E(m,\pi)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}(n,m,\pi)\mathcal{F}_{G}(f)(n,m,\pi)

and

\mathcal{F}_{G}(\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}^{G}f)(n,m,\pi):=\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\leqslant E(n,\pi)\leqslant 2^{k}\}}(n,m,\pi)\mathcal{F}_{G}(f)(n,m,\pi).

Thus Proposition
4.1.3
, Items 1, 2, 3, 5, 6 and 7, Proposition
4.1.4
, Items 1, 2, 3, 5, 6 and 7, and Proposition
4.2.1
, Items 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8 and 9 can be generalized to
\mathsf{J}_{k}^{G}
,
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}^{G}
and
\mathbb{P}^{G}
. Furthermore, we can adapt the strategy of Section
4
to System (
9.4
) in order to derive the following approximate problems depending on
k\in\mathbb{N}
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{k}-\nu\mathbb{P}^{G}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G}u_{k}+\mathbb{P}^{G}\mathsf{J}_{k}^{G}(u_{k}\cdot\nabla_{G}\mathsf{J}_{k}^{G}u_{k})=0\ &\text{in}\ \ (0,+\infty)\times G,\\
\divergence_{G}(u_{k})=0\ &\text{in}\ \ (0,+\infty)\times G,\\
\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}^{G}u_{k}=u_{k}\ &\text{in}\ \ (0,+\infty)\times G\\
\end{cases}

(9.5)

and

u_{k|_{t=0}}=\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}^{G}u_{0}\ \text{in}\ \ G,

(9.6)

where
u_{0}
is a horizontal vector field belonging to
L^{2}(G)^{N^{\prime}}
or
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(G)^{N^{\prime}}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, the solution
u_{k}
belongs to
L^{2}(L^{2})\cap L^{2}(\dot{H}^{1}(G))\cap\mathcal{C}_{b}(\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}}(G))
with
\nabla_{G}u_{k}\in L^{2}(\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}}(G))
, for any
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and satisfies

\|u_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(L^{2})}^{2}+2\nu\|\nabla_{G}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(L^{2})}^{2}\leqslant\|\tilde{\mathsf{J}}_{k}^{G}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(9.7)

Open problems.

We end this section with some open questions related to (
9.2
)-(
9.3
).

A first open problem concerns the global well-posedness in the case of a general stratified Lie group
G
for initial data in the critical space
\tilde{H}^{Q/2-1}(G)
.
Indeed, when the homogeneous dimension is odd, this imposes to work with fractional order Sobolev spaces. In such case, estimating the nonlinear terms is challenging because we need to simultaneously address the regularity associated with both the right-invariant and left-invariant sub-Laplacians on
G
(in this article, since
Q/2-1=d\in\mathbb{N}
when
G=\mathbb{H}^{d}
, we only need to use the Leibniz formula and the Sobolev’s embedding.)

A second question concerns the regularity of the solution. In particular, when the step of
G
is strictly larger than
2
, the interplay between the different stratums of vector fields is not so clear. Therefore, the regularization properties of the solutions of (
9.2
)-(
9.3
) become much more difficult to analyze. Indeed, looking at the proof of Theorem
1.2.5
, we compensate the loss of derivatives due to the degenerate diffusion operator
(\Id-\mathbb{P}^{G})\circ\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{G}
by a regularity of infinite order in the other directions. Such strategy is made possible in the case of the Heisenberg group by the crucial facts that a parametrization of
\widehat{G}
is simply given by
\mathbb{R}^{*}
, and that we have a spectral gap
in the sense of Proposition
2.4.8
, revealed by the analysis of the symbol of the sub-Laplacian (see also
[
5
]
in the context of the Engel group).

Appendix A
A result of anisotropic analytic hypoellipticity of the fractional power of the sub-Laplacian on
\mathbb{H}^{d}

In this section we state and prove a result which interprets the radius of analyticity with respect to the vertical variable in the framework of the homogeneous Sobolev-type space
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(the same result holds if we replace
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
by
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
, with the suitable modifications):

Theorem A.0.1
.

Let
\ell>0
. Let
f\in\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\sigma>0
. If
e^{\sigma|D_{s}|}f\in\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, then there exist two functions
g
and
h

satisfying

f=g+h

such that

1.

g
belongs to
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
and for any
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, the function
g(Y,\cdot)
can be extended to a holomorphic function on
\mathbb{C}
,

2.

h
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and for any
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, the function
h(Y,\cdot)
can be extended to a holomorphic function in
\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}:=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}~\middle|~|\Im(z)|<\sigma\right\}
.

In particular,
f
is analytic with respect to the vertical variable
s
.

Let us remark that this theorem implies partial (and global) analytic hypoellipticity properties for
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}
. Indeed, if
f
is a complex value smooth function on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, such that
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}f
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and, for any
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, the function
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}f(Y,\cdot)
extends to a holomorphic function on
\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}
satisfying

\sup_{|\eta|<\sigma}\{\|(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}f(Y,\cdot+i\eta)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\}<+\infty,

then we have
e^{\sigma|D_{s}|}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{\ell}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(this can be done for instance by adapting the proof of
[
2
, Theorem 1.1]
to the case of
\mathbb{R}
). It then follows from Theorem
A.0.1
that
f(Y,\cdot)
is analytic on
\mathbb{R}
and extends to a holomorphic function on
\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}
.

Additionally, let us point out that, in the case of
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, the exponential decay of the Fourier transform does not imply analyticity. Indeed, there exists a smooth function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
such that the support of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)
is included in the set

\left\{(n,m,\lambda)~\middle|~4|\lambda|(2|n|+d)\leqslant R\right\}

for some
R>0
, and which cannot be extended into an analytic function on
\mathbb{C}^{2d+1}
(see
[
6
]
).

Proof of Theorem
A.0.1
.

Let
\psi_{1}
be a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
with value in
[0,1]
and satisfying
supp(\psi_{1})\subset(-2,2)
and
\psi_{1}=1
on
[-1,1]
. Let us set
\psi_{2}:=1-\psi_{1}
. Since
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are bounded, thanks to Proposition
B.0.1
, we can write

f=\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f+\psi_{2}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f.

Because
\psi_{1}
is compactly supported smooth function, we have
\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
(use Proposition
B.0.1
to ensure that this function belongs to
\tilde{H}^{\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
for any
\ell^{\prime}\in[\ell,+\infty)
and the Sobolev embedding in order to recover the continuity of all derivative with respect to the right-invariant vector fields). Besides,
according to the Hulanicki theorem (see
[
19
, Corollary 4.5.2, p. 252]
), the operators
\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})
maps
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Assume that
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. In this case
\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and we have (see Formula (
2.20
))

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}\left(\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f\right)(n,m,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\{|\lambda|\leqslant 2\}}(n,m,\lambda)\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}\left(\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f\right)(n,m,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{|D_{s}|\leqslant 2\}}\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f\right)(n,m,\lambda).

According to the Fourier inversion formula in Proposition
2.2.1
, we thus get

\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f=\mathbf{1}_{\{|D_{s}|\leqslant 2\}}\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f.

Since
\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})
and
\mathbf{1}_{\{|D_{s}|\leqslant 2\}}\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})
are bounded operators on
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, by using the density of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
in
\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we deduce that the above formula holds for any
f\in\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Then for any
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, the Fourier transform on
\mathbb{R}
of
\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f(Y,\cdot)
is compactly supported in
\mathbb{R}
. Then according to the Paley-Wiener theorem, we deduce that
\psi_{1}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f(Y,\cdot)
extends to a holomorphic function on
\mathbb{C}
.

On the other hand, since
supp(\psi_{2})\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
and
\|\psi_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 1
, for any
(n,m,\lambda)\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}
, we have

|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}\left(\psi_{2}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})e^{\sigma|D_{s}|}f\right)(n,m,\lambda)|\leqslant(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{\ell}|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}\left(e^{\sigma|D_{s}|}f\right)(n,m,\lambda)|.

Thus, using that
e^{\sigma|D_{s}|}f\in\tilde{H}^{\ell}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we deduce that

e^{\sigma|D_{s}|}\psi_{2}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f=\psi_{2}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})e^{\sigma|D_{s}|}f
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Then, we deduce that for any
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, the function
\psi_{2}(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f(Y,\cdot)
can be extended to a holomorphic function with respect to the variable
s
in
\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}
.
∎

Appendix B
Symbol of the negative powers of the sub-Laplacian

Our first goal is to prove the following proposition, which is deduced by suitably combining several results and arguments scattered in
[
19
]
:

Proposition B.0.1
.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, the infinitesimal representation
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})
of
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
is given by

\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})=-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda}\ \text{ where }-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda}:=4\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}-\partial_{x_{j}}^{2}+|\lambda|^{2}x_{j}^{2}\right),

and the space of smooth vector fields of
(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda},L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Moreover, for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathbb{R})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})

(B.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})\circ\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}).

(B.2)

where
\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})
and
\varphi(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})
are bounded self-adjoint operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and

\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda}):=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varphi(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))\mathcal{P}_{m,\lambda},

(B.3)

where
\mathcal{P}_{m,\lambda}:=\langle\cdot,h_{m,\lambda}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}h_{m,\lambda}
is a bounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

If
\varphi
is a measurable function on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that there exist positive constants
C
and
\ell\in[0,+\infty)
such that

\forall\mu\in(0,\infty),\quad|\varphi(\mu)|\leqslant C(1+|\mu|)^{\ell},

(B.4)

then formula (
B.1
) and (
B.2
) hold for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

Remark B.0.2
.

Let us point out that in
[
19
]
(see
[
19
, Proposition 1.7.6]
where it is given for
\varphi(x)=x
), the formula (
B.1
) and (
B.2
) are inverted due to the difference of convention: in
[
19
]
the authors define the Fourier transform as
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}f(w)\mathrm{U}_{-w}^{\lambda}dw
while, we use
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}f(w)\mathrm{U}_{w}^{\lambda}dw
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
. For any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
j\in[\![1,d]\!]
, we have
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}_{\exp_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}(tX_{j})}\varphi(x)=\varphi(x-2te_{j})
and
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}_{\exp_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}(t\Xi_{j})}\varphi(x)=e^{-2i\lambda tx_{j}}\varphi(x)
, we deduce that the values of the infinitesimal
representation (see
[
19
, Proposition 1.7.3, p. 38]
) associated to
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}
in
X_{j}
and
\Xi_{j}
are respectively
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(X_{j})=-2\partial_{x_{j}}
and
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(\Xi_{j})=-2i\lambda x_{j}
. We thus deduce that, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, we have

\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})=-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(X_{j})^{2}+\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(\Xi_{j})^{2}\right)=-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda}.

(B.5)

If
\varphi
belongs to
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, then
\varphi
is bounded on the spectrum
\mathrm{Sp}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})=\{4|\lambda|(2|m|+d)\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}
of
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda}
, and consequently Formula (
B.3
) defines a bounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
\varphi
be a real-valued function on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. We now prove (
B.1
) and (
B.2
). Thanks to the spectral theorem for unbounded self-adjoint operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, the operators
\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})
and
\varphi(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})
are bounded self-adjoint operators since
\varphi
is real valued and bounded on the spectrum of
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
. Moreover, it is easy to check that
\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})
and
\varphi(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})
are respectively left-invariant and right-invariant operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Thus according to the Schwartz kernel theorem on Lie group (see for instance
[
19
, Corollary 3.2.1, p. 133]
), we deduce that there exist
\kappa_{\varphi}^{r}
and
\kappa_{\varphi}^{\ell}
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
such that

\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})f=f\star\kappa_{\varphi}^{\ell}\ \text{and}\ \varphi(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f=\kappa_{\varphi}^{r}\star f,

(B.6)

where
\star
denotes the (non-commutative) convolution product on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
extended to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and define for any functions
a
and
b
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
by

(a\star b)(Y,s):=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{d}}a(Y-Y^{\prime},s-s^{\prime}-\langle\mathfrak{S}Y,Y^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}})b(Y^{\prime},s^{\prime})dY^{\prime}ds^{\prime},\quad\text{ for }(Y,s)\in\mathbb{H}^{d}.

For any
a\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we denote
\check{a}:=a(-\cdot)
(in the sense of distributions). Since
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}f=(\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\check{f})(-\cdot)
for any
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, we deduce that the spectral measures
E
and
\tilde{E}
corresponding to
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
are linked by the relation
\tilde{E}f=(E\check{f})(-\cdot)
, by the uniqueness of the spectral measure of
-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}}
.
Then, up to a suitable approximation, we deduce that for any
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
(
\varphi
is real valued) and
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
, that

\varphi(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f=(\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})\check{f})(-\cdot).

Thus, thanks to (
B.6
), we deduce from the above identity that
\kappa_{\varphi}^{r}\star f=(\check{f}\star\kappa_{\varphi}^{\ell})(-\cdot)=\check{\kappa}_{\varphi}^{\ell}\star f
, and by the uniqueness of the Schwartz kernel theorem, we deduce that

\kappa_{\varphi}^{r}=\check{\kappa}_{\varphi}^{\ell}.

(B.7)

By applying the Fourier transform on the two identities in (
B.6
), thanks to the abstract Plancherel theorem on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
(see for instance
[
19
, Theorem 1.8.11, p. 52]
) (keeping in mind that
f\mapsto f\star\kappa_{\varphi}^{\ell}
and
f\mapsto\kappa_{\varphi}^{r}\star f
are bounded operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
), we deduce that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi(-{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\kappa_{\varphi}^{\ell})(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}),

(B.8)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\check{\kappa}_{\varphi}^{\ell})(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}).

(B.9)

Following the same lines as in the proof of
[
19
, Corollary 4.1.16, Identity (4.5), p. 179]
, we deduce the formula

\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\check{\kappa}_{\varphi}^{\ell})(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda}).

The first step of this proof consists of applying formula (
B.9
) up to a suitable approximation
\varphi=\Id_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
and
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Then, according to the Dixmier-Malliavin theorem (see
[
19
, Theorem 1.7.8, p. 42]
), the uniqueness of the spectral measure of
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})
and the identity
\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})=-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda}
in (
B.5
), we deduce the above identity.

Then, since
\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\kappa_{\varphi}^{\ell})(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})^{*}=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\check{\kappa}_{\varphi}^{\ell})(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})
and
\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})
is self-adjoint, we deduce from the above identity that

\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\kappa_{\varphi}^{\ell})(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\varphi(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\check{\kappa}_{\varphi}^{\ell})(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\kappa_{\varphi}^{r})(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}).

(B.10)

The identities (
B.1
) and (
B.2
) then follow from (
B.8
)-(
B.9
) and (
B.10
). The fact that these identities can be extended to any
\varphi
satisfying (
B.4
) can be done as in
[
19
, Example 5.1.27, p. 291]
.
∎

According to Proposition
B.0.1
, if
\varphi(\mu)=\mu^{\ell}
for
\ell\geqslant 0
, then (
B.1
) and (
B.2
) hold for every
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. This is no longer the case when
\ell<0
, which deserves some special attention:

Lemma B.0.3
.

Let
\ell>0
. If
f
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell})
, respectively
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell})
, then for almost every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
we have

\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})^{-\ell},

and if
f
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell})
, then for almost every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
we have

\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}((-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})^{-\ell}\circ\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda}).

Proof.

Let
\ell>0
and
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})\cap\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell})
. For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
\chi_{j}:=\mathbf{1}_{\{\mu^{-\ell}\leqslant 2^{j}\}}
and we define the function
\varphi_{j}
by setting, for any
\mu>0
,

\varphi_{j}(\mu):=\chi_{j}(\mu)\mu^{-\ell}.

Since
\varphi_{j}
is a bounded measurable function, according to Proposition
B.0.1
, we have

\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi_{j}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ\varphi_{j}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda}).

(B.11)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ

\displaystyle\varphi_{j}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})-\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})^{-\ell}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2d}}|\langle\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ\left(\varphi_{j}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})-(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})^{-\ell}\right)h_{m,\lambda},h_{n,\lambda}\rangle_{L^{2}}|^{2}.

Besides, thanks to the functional calculus of
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda}
(see Proposition
B.0.1
), for any
m\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we have

\varphi_{j}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})h_{m,\lambda}=\varphi_{j}(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))h_{m,\lambda}\text{ and }(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})^{-\ell}h_{m,\lambda}=(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{-\ell}h_{m,\lambda}.

Thus

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ

\displaystyle\varphi_{j}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})-\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})^{-\ell}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2d}}\left(\varphi_{j}(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))-(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{-\ell}\right)^{2}|\langle\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})h_{m,\lambda},h_{n,\lambda}\rangle_{L^{2}}|^{2}.

Furthermore, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
(m,\lambda)\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\varphi_{j}(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))-(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{-\ell}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{(0,2^{-j/\ell})}\left(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d)\right)(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{-2\ell}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2^{-\frac{j}{\ell}}}{(4|\lambda|(2|m|+d))^{2\ell+1}}\leqslant\frac{2^{-\frac{j}{\ell}}}{|\lambda|^{2\ell+1}}.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ\varphi_{j}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})-\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ(-

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})^{-\ell}\|_{\mathrm{HS}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2^{-j/\ell}}{|\lambda|^{2\ell+1}}\|\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}.

(B.12)

The quantity in the right-hand side of the above inequality is finite because
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
implies that
\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\in\mathrm{HS}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(see Proposition
2.2.1
). Combining (
B.1
) and (
B.2
), we deduce that

\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*},\ \ \lim_{j\rightarrow+\infty}\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi_{j}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})^{-\ell}.

(B.13)

Moreover, on one hand, since
f\in\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell})
, we have
(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. On the other hand, using that
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
and
\varphi_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we get
\varphi_{j}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. Then, we deduce from the Plancherel theorem on
\mathbb{H}^{d}
(see Proposition
2.2.1
) and the functional calculus for
-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}
that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{*}}\|\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(\varphi_{j}(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})-

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\|_{\mathrm{HS}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}|\lambda|^{d}d\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{d+1}}{2^{d-1}}\|\mathbf{1}_{(0,2^{-j/\ell})}\left(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}}\right)\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{d+1}}{2^{d-1}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathsf{1}_{(0,2^{-j/\ell})}(\mu)|\mu|^{-2\ell}d\langle E(\mu)f,f\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})}.

Since
f\in\mathrm{Dom}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell})
, we have
|\cdot|^{-2\ell}\in L^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+},d\langle Ef,f\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{d})}\right)
. Then, according to the dominated convergence theorem, the right-hand side of the above identity converges to
0
when
j
goes to
+\infty
. Hence, we deduce that

a.\ e.\ \lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*},\ \ \ \mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}((-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})=\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{H}}(f)(\mathrm{U}^{\lambda})\circ(-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{osc}^{\lambda})^{-\ell},

in
\mathrm{HS}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(the class of Hilbert-Schmidt operators in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
). The case of
(-\tilde{\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}}_{\mathbb{H}})^{-\ell}
follows similarly (see Proposition
B.0.1
).
∎

Appendix C
Compactness results for sub-elliptic Sobolev spaces

We have the following Rellich-type theorem.

Proposition C.0.1
.

Let
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
be two real numbers such that
\ell<\ell^{\prime}
and
p\in(1,+\infty)
. The multiplication by any element of
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
is a compact operator from
W^{\ell^{\prime},p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
W^{\ell/2,p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. The multiplication operator by
\varphi
denoted by
M_{\varphi}
is a bounded linear operator from
W_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell^{\prime},p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
W^{\ell^{\prime}/2,p}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
(see
[
19
, Theorem 4.4.24, p. 240]
). Besides, the multiplication by a smooth compactly supported function is a compact operator from
W^{\ell^{\prime}/2,p}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
to
W^{\ell/2,p}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
and a bounded operator from
W^{\ell/2,p}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
to
W^{\ell/2,p}_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
(see
[
19
, Theorem 4.4.24, p. 240]
). Consequently,
M_{\varphi}
is a compact operator from
W_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell^{\prime},p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
to
W_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ell/2,p}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
.
∎
